# Concept map: Rajnish Choubey's "Introduction, Part 1" (Chapters 1-3) -> AXI

Sources (thecinderellaproject8.wordpress.com): Chapters 1-3 (26 Jul 2026: "The Super 4", "The Multi-Dimensional Shield", "The Open
Architecture of N"), Chapter 4 "The Zero-Preference Mirror" (26 Jul), "The Geometry of a Clean Slate" (29 Jul, ~9,000 words), and the
"civilizational checkmate" post (28 Jul). Not usable: the post titled "366" (an image only); "The Cinderella Project is an avant-garde
digital movement..." could not be fetched (its address is too long for the fetch tool, and the site's public API disallows automated
access, so I did not go around it) - paste its text if it matters.

## Read this first

* The chapters contain **no equations or definitions of computation**; the one passage with real mathematics (the snake / "infinite
  remainder" in "The Geometry of a Clean Slate") is about discretizing continuous motion, and it turned out to be the most productive
  source (rows 16-20). They are a worldview and a
  design philosophy told through archetypes. The mathematics used in the paper (DPO gluing conditions, adhesive categories, D4
  orbits) is **not in these pages**; it must come from elsewhere in Rajnish's work.
* Everything in the "Formal reading" and "Where in AXI" columns is **my proposed mapping**, not something the text says. Rajnish
  should confirm, correct or reject each row before any of it is attributed to him.
* "Supported" means we hold measurements consistent with the reading. It does not mean the text predicted them; the readings were
  made after the data existed.
* The end of Chapter 3 addresses "Rajnish" directly and offers "Select your coordinate". It reads like a conversation with an AI
  assistant pasted as a post. His own notes or prompts would be a better source for the original ideas than the polished prose.

## 1. Mapping table

Status: **SUPPORTED** = our data agrees · **MAPS** = a real object in the code, tested · **TEST** = proposed experiment, not run ·
**TENSION** = the reading conflicts with what we built · **INTERP** = worldview, nothing to map or test

| # | His words | Formal reading | Where in AXI | Evidence | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Intelligence is "subtractive, relational, and cyclical", not accumulative (Ch.1) | subtractive = remove options (logit mask); relational = admissibility depends on edges, not tokens (gluing conditions); cyclical = propose -> gate -> feedback loop | CICO DFA mask; DPO gate; retry loop | mask lifts unconstrained strict-parse 91.7% -> 100% (83.3% -> 100% on delete_dep); mask alone leaves 100% corruption on delete_dep; gate -> 0% corruption; adding the loop -> 100% success | **SUPPORTED** (all three words map to a measured component) |
| 2 | Durga = structure, mass, "protective geometry"; the *Shirorekha*, the line that disciplines the floating curves of language | the syntactic layer: a grammar that anchors free-form generation | CICO grammar DFA + token masker | necessary (parse failures vanish) but not sufficient: this is exactly the Syntax Fallacy | **MAPS** |
| 3 | Kali swallows Raktabeeja's blood "before it can touch the ground" | transaction abort: speculative state is discarded before it commits | copy-on-write pager `rollback`; cross-rank all-or-nothing | pager audit `free + mapped + in_flight == capacity` after every step; 4 real processes, 2,066 aborts / 934 commits, identical page tables, no leaks | **MAPS** |
| 4 | Buddha = clean slate, middle path, vertical leap out of "additive noise" | reset to the last committed state; minimal edit | rollback to last commit; `propose_repair` (adds only the edges that are required, or drops the node delete) | repair is minimal and sound (4,000-case fuzz); "middle path" is a loose fit | **MAPS** (clean slate) / weak (middle path) |
| 5 | Shiva = Shunya, the zero point; "Nirvana is Shunya"; when the two masters become indistinguishable "the heavy hierarchy collapses" | quotient: different representations identified with one canonical form | D4 canonicalization (8 orientations -> one representative) | canonical task and prompt text byte-identical for all 8 orientations (`tests/test_d4.py`) | **MAPS** (best fit in the whole text) |
| 6 | The Super 4 together are the "ceiling of protection": fortress + incinerator + clarity + stillness | layered defence; each layer has a different job | grammar / gate+pager / repair / canonical form | ablation: grammar alone 0% success, 100% corrupted; + gate 0% corrupted but still 0% success; + feedback 100% success. In systems terms: grammar = parse validity, gate = **safety** (nothing bad commits), retry/repair = **liveness** (something good eventually happens) | **SUPPORTED** (the safety/liveness split is my addition) |
| 7 | Western monotheism = "singular, paternal authority of absolute law", structurally fragile; 'N' gods = "zero point of failure" (Ch.2) | one rule stated in the prompt vs several independent guards | `--hint-rules` experiment vs the layered wrapper | rule in the prompt: imperative retry 100% -> 33.3% / 30.0% on delete_dep (2 seeds); layered wrapper 100% / 100% | **SUPPORTED** for "one rule is fragile" (3B model, one task family only) |
| 8 | "Zero point of failure: distributed consciousness" | redundancy / fault tolerance | Layer 3 consensus | **our commit is all-or-nothing: every rank has a veto.** That is fail-safe, not fault-tolerant; a crashed rank blocks commit. Redundancy would need quorum | **TENSION** |
| 9 | n+1 accumulation (more parameters) is a "linear trap"; the open architecture of N adds without anxiety (Ch.3) | structure beats scale | wrapper vs a bigger unwrapped model | partial: with Qwen2.5-0.5B the wrapper had nothing to repair (it never wrote a plain delete), so structure does **not** replace a minimum capability. **Compared 8 Oct against Qwen2.5-7B (4-bit), seed 3** (`SCALE_TEST_7B.md`): unwrapped, the 7B corrupts 30/30 delete-with-dependents tasks without the rule (3B: 30/30), so scale alone did not fix it on this task; every gated mode 0 %. One family, one seed, 4-bit against fp16 | **TEST DONE (one size, one seed)** |
| 10 | "Cleaning the internal blackboard" / wipe the slate each morning | stateless reset | abort -> last committed state | same evidence as row 3 | **MAPS** (duplicate of 3/4) |
| 11 | "You hold the keyboard, you define the variables" (Ch.3) | user-defined vocabulary / schema | AXI-DSL idea (MATCH / PRESERVE / PRODUCE spans) | not built | **INTERP** (possible later) |
| 12 | Radha, Calvin & Hobbes, Krishna, Saraswati, Lakshmi, Annapurna, Manakamana, Gahwa Mai | worldview / personal pantheon | none | none; nothing to test | **INTERP** |

Also reconciles row 8: **plural at the proposal level (N proposers), single anchor at the commit level** - exactly the AXI shape. The anchor is then a single point of failure (fail-safe, not fault-tolerant) |

(An identical re-add is silently dropped by `normalize()`.) | the benchmark has no update tasks; the draft paper's "attribute patches" cannot be expressed at all | **FINDING (verified)**. If Rajnish means one class (a compass: N, NE, E ...), the symmetry is a 45-degree rotation of the compass, which is not a symmetry of a square grid and is not what the code does. "Nine is closed" has no mechanism: the benchmark is 3x3 because it is small, and nothing here shows the approach holds on larger graphs (row 40 is the test) | **MAPS** (centre and orbits, tested); his meaning of "gate" is **question 18** |

The repo works at this interface only; it never looks inside the model | rows 1 and 16: the mask lifts strict parse from 91.7% to 100%, and the gate catches what a parseable string still gets wrong (silent failures) | **MAPS** (framing). Not built and not measured: the "12,888-dimensional manifold" and any steering of the geometry (posts 86, 87, Medium "AI Mapped"). ## 1c. Provenance note

You said the earlier technical files came from texts like these. That fits what we found: the formal drafts (AXI-DSL, the Qwen patch,
the latency table, the theorem) read as an AI's formalization of this philosophy. They are best treated as hypotheses generated from
Rajnish's ideas, which is how they have been handled: each one either got a test (and a number) or was marked unmeasured. The
ideas are his; the formal drafts were AI-assisted; the verification is the part that is new.

### Caution about the pasted Cinderella Project text
It reads like an AI-search summary (bracketed citations `[1, 2, 3]` that all point to generic site addresses), not the original articles.
It names you, Rajnish and Rahul Chauhan and attributes roles and personal details to each. None of that is verified, and the
"100+ day protocol" and the five challenges are second-hand. Do not cite it in the paper or README; use the original Medium
article ("The Delete Test") if it exists. It also shows two stances to reconcile before publishing: the project's framing ("make the
machine fail, show it is not alive") and the paper's ("a deterministic substrate for agent runtimes"). They fit together as one
sentence the data supports: *we do not need the model to be right; we need the system to be safe when it is wrong.*

## 2. What the text does give us (usable as stated, attributed to Rajnish)

Three design principles that our results agree with. They can go in a README "Design philosophy" section as Rajnish's framing:
1. **Subtract, relate, cycle.** Constrain by removing options, judge by relations rather than tokens, and close the loop with feedback.
2. **Several independent protections, not one rule.** No single layer was enough in our data.
3. **Always be able to return to a clean state.** Speculative work is cheap to discard; committed state is never half-written.

Keep the archetype names out of the technical body of the preprint (credibility rests on the measured claims). If Rajnish wants
them, a short clearly-labelled "Motivation" section is the safe place.

## 3. Proposed tests that come out of the mapping

1. **Structure vs scale (row 9).** Same 60-task benchmark with a larger model (Qwen2.5-7B in 4-bit on the T4) unwrapped vs
   wrapped. Prediction to falsify: a bigger model alone still corrupts the graph on delete-with-dependents tasks. If it does not,
   the "structure beats scale" reading is much weaker than it looks.
   **Run 8 Oct (7B, 4-bit, seed 3):** prediction and reading rule were fixed in advance in `SCALE_TEST_7B.md`; result there (a "Result" section): blind apply corrupts 30/30 `delete_dep` tasks without the rule, so by the rule fixed in advance scale alone did not fix it on this task; every gated mode 0 %. With the rule stated the 7B over-applies it on tasks that do not need it, and the gate (a legality check) admits 7 of the 12 such wrong deletes. Notebook `python/colab/axi_7b_test.ipynb`, helpers `python/colab/colab_scale_run.py`, runner `python/axi/experiments/resumable.py`; tests `test_resumable_run.py`, `test_scale_run_helpers.py`, `test_scale_result_7b.py`. Open: a fresh seed, 1.5B and fp16 comparisons, the scope-containment idea in section 4 of that page.
2. **Safety vs liveness (row 6).** Already measurable from existing logs: report corruption (safety) and success (liveness) as
   two separate columns for every mode. This is what the paper tables already do; it only needs the two names.
3. **Quorum commit (row 8).** If fault tolerance is wanted, a commit that tolerates one failed rank is a different protocol and
   would need its own tests.

84. **Keep only the changes the request names (rows 3, 9, 27)** - **run 8 Oct, all 13 pre-registered reading-rule lines yes**. The long-horizon test left one hole: a change that is legal but was not asked for (the 7B told the rule deletes real edges nobody asked it to delete; M6 stayed at 55 % exact). `scope_m7` reads the coordinates written in the request, drops every change that touches anything else, then runs the M6 path. With the rule stated it took the 7B from 118 to 134 correct graphs of 140 (harmed none) and from 73.1 % to 98.3 % exact steps over 30 requests in a row; with no rule it changed nothing. The post-hoc version of the idea (`scope_delta`) solved 0 of 60 per regime on the new request kinds, as predicted. The remaining misses are all "remove every edge with weight w" (no service named) and one wrong-source edge, which a coordinate filter cannot fix. Open: requests in ordinary words. Prediction and rules in `SCOPE_TEST.md`; `python/axi/engine/scope.py`, `python/axi/experiments/{scope_tasks,scope_test}.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_scope_test.ipynb`, instrument `tests/test_scope.py`, result check `tests/test_scope_result.py`.
85. **Does it still work on a bigger model? (rows 3, 9, 27)** - **run 9 Oct: 14 of 15 pre-registered reading-rule lines yes (all 10 [guard] lines, 4 of 5 [need] lines)**. Qwen2.5-14B in 4-bit, the same 140 tasks and the same first episodes as the 7B scope test. The guard did not weaken: gate, M6 and scope_m7 corrupted no graph in 840 single requests and no step in 600 long steps. The need got smaller but did not go away: blind apply still corrupted 24 of 140 graphs without the rule (7B 36) and 17 with it (7B 24), and every one of the 10 long episodes. The one "no" is a single task (no rule, blind apply right on 11 of 20 delete_dep requests, line said 10 or fewer; the 7B was right on 0). Measured gains: M6 over blind apply 24 tasks (7B 36); scope_m7 over M6 with the rule stated 16 tasks (7B 16) in single requests, 28 steps of 180 (7B 56) over 30 in a row; scope_m7 exact on 180 of 180 and 120 of 120 long steps. Unchanged by size: the service delete with a separate edge delete (4 of 20), stray edge deletes with the rule stated (16 tasks), the 11 `delete_weight` misses. The 14B took 2.3 times (no rule) and 1.7 times (rule) the 7B's time per request; the filter about 13 microseconds. Limits: one family, one size step, 4-bit, greedy, coordinates only, 6 and 4 episodes, two Colab sessions. Prediction, reading rules and the written Result in `SCALE_TEST_14B.md`; `python/axi/experiments/bigger_model.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_14b_test.ipynb`, instrument `tests/test_bigger_model.py`, result check `tests/test_bigger_model_result.py` = test 88 (every number recomputed from the four saved files in `docs/results/`; 41 of 41 changes caught). The first run was stopped by Colab's usage limit in the middle of Part 2; `python/colab/axi_14b_part2.ipynb` (Part 2 only, progress saved to Google Drive after every finished episode, `python/colab/colab_persist.py`, `tests/test_colab_persist.py` = test 87, 47 of 47 changes caught) was built for that; in the end Part 2 was simply run again from its start with the first notebook, so the resumable notebook was not needed for this result. The test itself was never made smaller.
86. **Does it still work on a model that Google serves, on the free tier? (rows 3, 9, 27)** - **Part A run 9 to 10 Oct 2026 on Gemini 3.5 Flash Lite; Part B (3.8 Flash, the 20 `delete_dep` requests) not run yet.** The same 140 tasks and the same 6 and 4 episodes as the 14B, through a free key; replies sampled, one per distinct prompt, every reply saved. Prediction and reading rules fixed first (`API_TEST_GEMINI.md`, locked by a hash test; two dated addenda about busy-server handling, no task, prompt or rule changed); adapter `api_llm.py`, new reading rules `api_test.py`, notebook `axi_gemini_test.ipynb`, instrument `tests/test_api_gemini.py` = test 89 (103 of 103 deliberate changes caught), result check `tests/test_api_gemini_result.py` = test 90 (15 of 15 caught). **Result: the new [format] line failed (78 of 651 replies do not parse, 12 %, one slip `DEL[2,0:7]`, 75 of them rule-stated), so the rule-stated regime says nothing about the framework on this model. With no rule stated and a clean format, repair_m6 and scope_m7 corrupted nothing and held the intended state on all 120 long-run steps; blind apply held it on 50.8 % (3 of 4 episodes corrupted at some point; the prediction of 2 of 4 by step 10 was missed, it was 1 of 4); single requests 135 of 140 against 120, with 15 graphs corrupted by blind apply, all `delete_pair`. Blind apply was right on 20 of 20 `delete_dep` (prediction missed): this model needs the framework less on that request than the 7B and 14B did.** 16 reading-rule lines, 9 yes, 7 no. Exploratory re-read after the files were seen: with the number dropped, rule stated gives blind 137 / framework 138 of 140, no rule blind 123 / framework 138. Limits: Flash Lite is not a Pro or frontier model; the rule-stated regime and Part B are open; nothing here supports a statement about savings for AI firms.
87. **Can the scope filter read a request written in ordinary words? (rows 3, 9, 27)** - **built and tested without a model; NOT run on a model, so no word-request number exists yet.** The scope test (item 84) found what a request is about by reading coordinates written in the text; a person writes "remove the billing service". New module `python/axi/engine/grounding.py`: the host keeps a table of service names (and a few aliases); a name that appears as whole words is bound to its service (longest first), a leftover word that is part of exactly one name is bound, a phrase that fits two or more services is **ambiguous and applies nothing**, a phrase that fits none with nothing else named applies nothing. Hook: `admit_scoped(..., names=)` in `engine/scope.py`, policy `scope_words` in `experiments/cascade.py`. Test design (`WORDS_TEST.md`, fixed and hash-locked before any run): the same 140 graphs and correct changes as the scope test asked in words (`experiments/word_tasks.py`), plus 20 ambiguity requests (two services share their first word; the correct reaction is to change nothing); five policies on the same replies; seven reading-rule lines; nine written predictions (every line yes: about 7 %; the expected finding is no harm and no help on the 140, the gain being the refusal on ambiguity). Runner `experiments/word_test.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_words_test.ipynb` (320 calls of Gemini 3.5 Flash Lite, one day's free budget). Checks: `tests/test_grounding.py` = test 91 (7 tests; 840 generated requests over six seeds read exactly right, the 20 ambiguity requests of each of three seeds read as ambiguous with exactly the two services, a model that guesses one of the two changes the graph under blind, gate, repair_m6 and scope_m7 on all 20 and under scope_words on none); `tests/test_word_test.py` = test 92 (6 tests: stand-in models careful, sloppy, forgetful and slipping, the boundary of every threshold, page and notebook agree, page hash lock, notebook run end to end on a fake world); 52 of 52 deliberate changes to the code and notebook caught. Limits: the name table is given, not learned; the grounding is exact and word-overlap matching, **not language understanding**; a paraphrase outside its stop list is refused, so it over-refuses real requests until its vocabulary grows; three or four templates per kind; one model; "change nothing" as the right reaction to ambiguity is our choice (a deployed system might ask).
88. **An AI-written audit of five "missing components" (pasted 10 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - each point checked against the repo before anything was built. (1) Words to targets (a grounding step): true, and now built, item 87. (2) A typed subgraph matcher in front of the delta (find the part of the graph a request describes by its shape, not by its name): true and useful; **next to build, not built yet**. (3) A hyperbolic lattice: **parked**, the paste gives no definition we could test. (4) A Triton kernel splice (the gate inside the decode loop): **parked**, the repo copy holds no kernel, it needs a GPU, and the cost it would save is already measured on the crop rollback (0.3 to 0.8 ms). (5) Sweeps over model families and sizes: partly out of date (the 14B and Gemini Flash Lite are done, the 3B open); the paste's claim that the result "matches the frontier" on ARC cannot be pre-registered as written and is not claimed; its line that rule stuffing "degrades every size" is not shown by any of our runs and is not claimed. Counted as an input to the roadmap, not as a result.
89. **A second pasted audit: five latency and efficiency claims (10 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - each checked against the repo before anything was built. (1) Dropping a finished step from the model's memory ("KV compaction after commit"): the idea is real, and block independence is verified bit-exact on a toy model with the strict mask (numpy and T4, `test_block_independence.py`); a stock model was not trained with that mask, so for it dropping the interior is an approximation whose size nobody has measured, and the paste's "70 to 85 percent" and "O(|K|)" are not measured here. **This is the real next experiment; it needs Colab and is not built.** (2) The 0.65 density switch: contradicted by our own test (`test_wire.py`: the byte-count crossover of the frame is about 0.8 percent density, and the 0.65 rule would waste over 80 times the space at that density); a bit matrix also cannot carry the relation and weight of our edges. (3) Hooking the mask into decoding: already done in the local runs (`CICOProc` in `colab_llm_experiment.py`); the measured mask cost is 77 us per token at batch 1 on the T4, not 1.2 us; a native kernel stays parked. (4) Leaving empty cells out and canonical form: both already exist and are lossless (`d4.compile_stream`, `d4.canonicalize_task`); the equivariant-attention layer is not built (it needs training) and stays parked. (5) A binary frame over shared memory: the Python reference exists (`axi.wire`). **Points 4 and 5 were measured now, with the prediction fixed first (`EFFICIENCY_MEASURES.md`; code `python/axi/experiments/efficiency.py`; results in `docs/results/axi_efficiency_*.json`): the pattern predicted came out (transport yes, NO, NO, yes; ARC yes, yes, NO, NO, NO).** Per step, prompt text takes 7.9 us and a JSON round trip 15.9 us, against 0.51 s for one model call (0.0047 percent); the Python frame path takes 41.4 us (CRC-32C in Python), so the pasted "2 to 8 ms" and "under 350 ns" are not reproduced and the transport is not a latency lever in this loop. On the 400 ARC training tasks (3,004 prompt grids) the sparse text restores every grid and the canonical task prompt is identical for all 8 rotated/mirrored images of all 400 tasks (7.87 different prompts without it), but it is shorter than the plain grid on only 23.1 percent of grids, 80 percent shorter or more on 2.8 percent (paste: "often 80 to 90 percent"), and 2.62 times longer in total; it helps only below about one lit cell in five; an exploratory re-read with each grid's most frequent colour left out gives 24.7 percent, 2.6 percent and 1.99 times. Checks: `tests/test_efficiency.py` = test 93 (17 tests; 64 deliberate changes to the code, the page and the saved files with 63 caught, the one not caught swaps the two relation numbers everywhere and changes no behaviour), `tests/test_efficiency_result.py` = test 94 (7 tests; every number in the Result recomputed from the three files). Limits: Python reference only (the Rust crates are not in this repo copy and were not timed; no shared memory or second process), lengths in characters not tokens, colour 0 (or the most frequent colour) as "empty", training set only, and **a shorter text says nothing about whether a model solves the task better**.
90. **A confirmation test: do the first hosted-model findings repeat on tasks nobody has looked at, with error margins? (10 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - **built and tested without a model; NOT run, so no number exists yet.** Asked for after the first hosted run and the words test were ready: "let it be expensive, a full proof test". No test proves a framework in general; this is the strongest one the free key allows. Everything before it was read on 4 to 6 episodes and a few seeds, and the instrument was built while looking at them, so a finding could be an accident. The page `CONFIRM_TEST.md` (hash-locked, written before any run) fixes: the model (Gemini 3.5 Flash Lite); **fresh tasks** (280 single requests from `make_scope_tasks(280, 21)` and 30 long episodes of 30 requests from seed 21, each list fixed by a sha256 that a test recomputes; a test checks that none of them occurs under seeds 0, 1, 2, 3 or 7); **error margins on every number that matters** (95 percent Wilson interval for shares of episodes; a bootstrap that resamples whole episodes, 2,000 resamples, generator seed 20261010, for shares of steps and for the gap between two policies paired by episode); a **wasted-token share** (tokens spent on steps that start from a state already different from the oracle's); thirteen reading-rule lines tagged [guard], [need], [replicate] and [format]; fourteen written predictions (every line yes: about 12 percent; every [guard] and [format] line: about 40 percent). One prompt change: one worked example line, `remove the service at (2,1), which has no edges -> DEL[2,1]`, because 12 percent of the first run's replies were lost to the slip `DEL[2,0:7]`; it says "no edges" so that it does not teach the model to leave edges out. Cost: about 3,200 calls expected (rated 2,600 to 3,800 at 70 percent): about a week on the free key (480 calls a day, every cell stops by itself and carries on the next day), one sitting with billing switched on (`PAID = True`). Code `python/axi/experiments/confirm_test.py`; notebook `python/colab/axi_confirm_test.ipynb`; Colab zip `axi-colab-confirm.zip`. Checks: `tests/test_confirm_test.py` = test 95 (17 tests: the wrapper, the fixed task lists, Wilson and bootstrap at known values, the analysis on stand-in models, every reading rule at its boundary, the page hash, the notebook run end to end across a simulated week of daily stops; 118 deliberate changes to the code, the page and the notebook, all caught). Limits: one model; synthetic graph-edit requests in an invented language; the 30 episodes come from one generator, so the margins say how sure we can be about this generator and nothing about whether it is representative; one sample per prompt; a hosted model can change behind its name; thinking tokens are not counted; **nothing here supports a statement about savings for any company.** A second model can be run with the same notebook (it needs billing: the free 3.8 Flash quota is 20 calls a day) and is read separately.
91. **A third pasted audit: nine claims about what is missing, and three "next commits" (12 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - each checked against the repo; **nothing in this item was run on a model.** **Already done, or wrong (3):** words to coordinates (item 87: built and tested, test 91 and 92, waiting for its model run); a prefix mask inside the Hugging Face loop (`CICOProc` in `colab_llm_experiment.py` is a `LogitsProcessor` passed to `model.generate(logits_processor=...)` whenever `constrained` is set, so "the model generates unconstrained text and the gate checks afterwards" is wrong for the constrained runs; what is not in the loop is the device-side table of `gpu_mask.py`); "run the notebooks on Qwen2.5-3B and 14B" (done: 3B on four seeds, 7B, 14B, and two Gemini models; `SCALE_TEST_14B.md`, `API_TEST_GEMINI.md`). **Contradicted (1):** the 0.65 density cutover (item 89, point 2: the frame's byte-count crossover is about 0.8 percent density, and text plus JSON per step is 0.0047 percent of one model call). **Half right (1):** "the D4 module is completely disconnected from ARC" - `efficiency.py arc` runs `d4.compile_stream` and `d4.canonicalize_task` over the 400 ARC training tasks (the canonical prompt is identical for all 8 images on 400 of 400), but **no model has been run on ARC with canonical prompts**: that is true, it is an untested question (does a model answer better, or at least consistently, on the canonical form?), and it would need a model run. **Real and already known (1):** splicing the pager and block mask into a stock model's KV cache (the lever of item 89, point 1): not built; this sandbox cannot install torch, so the plumbing could not be tested here; reusing the stale key/value blocks of unchanged text after editing one node is, on a model not trained with the strict mask, an approximation whose size nobody has measured. The paste's "5.1 times" and "memory error near 5,500 tokens" are the repo's own numbers (5.1 times by step 19; the memory error came at a 5,135-token prompt, `LONG_HORIZON_TEST.md`). **Needs training, cannot be checked, or parked (3):** spectral or random-walk coordinates as input embeddings (a hosted API takes text only, a local model would need training; the Rust crate that is said to compute them is not in this repo copy and could not be read); orthogonal coordinate and payload subspaces in the residual stream (needs training: an untrained projection would damage the model); the hyperbolic-to-lattice functor (mathematics only, no model experiment attached). **Not a gap in what we claim (1):** "multi-hop transitive scope" - deleting a service means removing it with its incident edges, one hop by definition; a cascade that also deletes the services depending on it would be a different request, and the scope filter would rightly drop it. **Found while checking it, and real (1): the repair keeps a service that has more than four connections instead of deleting it.** `propose_repair` closes the boundary of a deleted service by deleting its remaining edges (primal) up to `degree_threshold` = 4, and above that it drops the DEL of the service (dual: the service stays with all its edges). This was written in the module header from the start, it corrupts nothing, but **every task of every test so far deletes only services with at most three connections** (checked over the single requests of seeds 7 and 21 and the 30 long episodes of seed 21: connections 0 to 3, none above), so no model run and no reading rule ever reached the dual branch. Consequence: on a denser graph (a hub service), asked to remove the hub, repair_m6 and scope_m7 would leave it in place (well formed, not what was asked, "exact steps" would fall); blind apply would leave dangling edges. Pinned by `tests/test_hub_delete.py` = test 96 (7 tests: the gate refuses the bare delete at every size 1 to 10; the boundary is exactly 4 primal and 5 dual; the count is of the edges the reply left dangling; the same through `admit_scoped`; with `degree_threshold` large the intended state is reached at every size; the generators never reach it; two connected services deleted together; 16 of 16 deliberate changes caught). **Not decided:** whether to lift the threshold for a service the request names (my view: yes, the request itself authorises it; it would be a change of policy and a new pre-registered test on dense graphs with a model, not a silent edit of `scope_m7`, whose saved results are locked). Limits: synthetic graphs, no model, the tests show what the code does and not what a model would send. **Addendum (12 Oct): a fourth pasted text** (praise, then a wholesale replacement of `diagnostics.py`, `inverse.py` and `scope.py` by code written for plain integer node IDs). Checked, not applied. (a) Its two "bugs" do not exist here: connected services deleted together have each shared edge deleted once (edges are collected under the key from, to, relation), and `scope_m7` takes a set of named services; pinned by the seventh test. (b) The code does not fit: our edges carry a relation name that its edge key drops, and its `inverse.py` would replace the module that builds the inverse log (a different job, with its own tests); the `scope_m7` and `repair_m6` results are locked by saved files. (c) Its idea of an explicit CASCADE or RESTRICT declaration is sound and matches the open decision above; my reservation: cascading at any size is right for a service the request NAMES, not for one a model deleted by mistake (that is what the threshold was guarding), so the policy should be per named service, with a protected flag, and tested on dense graphs first. REWIRE (new edges around a deleted service) is not adopted: it invents edges, with no rule for their relation or weight. (d) "Zero corrupted graphs is the hardest milestone" overstates it: no dangling edge is a property of the checker, true for any model by construction (I rated it 99 percent before every run); what the runs measure is how often the held state equals the intended one. (e) "50.8 percent is the whole thesis" overstates it: 4 episodes of one model, and once a state is wrong it stays wrong, so the share mostly says when the first slip came; the confirmation test exists to put margins on it. (f) "Under 2 microseconds on an H100" is not measured here and is not to be written down; the 4 to 21 times is a T4 micro-benchmark against our own simple baseline, and the mask is not in the agent loop. (g) Correction to my own summary of 12 Oct: a service delete plus an unrelated edge delete (`delete_pair`) is FIXED by the framework (blind apply 3 to 4 of 20 right, repair_m6 and scope_m7 18 to 20 of 20, on the 7B and 14B in both regimes and on Flash Lite with no rule; Flash Lite with the rule stated is swamped by the format slip); the request kind the framework cannot help is a pure predicate with no named target (`delete_weight`: 13 to 18 of 20 for every policy, 2 to 7 misses per set, on all three models).
92. **Endurance and cost: one to two million tokens, the token bill, and what one wrong step costs (12 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - asked for a test of drift at one or two million tokens and of cost cutting. **"A million tokens" means two things.** A long chat: the context grows with the history and the bill with its square. A long job: the framework shows the model the exact state, so a step costs about the same all the way (Flash Lite: about 323 tokens, so 1 million tokens is about 3,100 steps, 2 million about 6,200). `ENDURANCE_TEST.md` (hash-locked page; `experiments/endurance.py`; tests 97 and 98) has three parts, **none of which calls a model**. (C, pre-registered, ten reading rules, all yes) The code between a reply and the held state, 1.6 million steps, 15 kinds of damaged reply: no exception, no corrupted state for the gate, repair_m6 and scope_m7, blind apply corrupted on 99.6 % of steps; scope_m7 flat at about 11 items from the oracle over two 250,000-step episodes, repair_m6 near 250, the gate alone growing (2,510 items). This tests the host code at scale; it says nothing about a model. (A, measured then extended) whole chat 304,289 tokens against 39,225 with the exact state, 105 steps (87.1 % fewer); the chat grows about 207 tokens a step; beyond 30 steps a straight line only; not compared with prefix caching or a summarised history. (B, replayed from the 8 saved runs, 0 mismatches in 4,800 steps) the cost of a wrong step: blind apply spent 89 % and 88 % of its tokens from an already wrong state, scope_m7 0 to 2 % in 5 of 6 settings; **the exception is Flash Lite with the rule stated, 88 % for every policy, because 42 of 180 replies were unreadable (`DEL[r,c:v]`) and never retried**. Later replies did not get worse after a mistake (10 of 11 comparable rows; post hoc): what persists is the wrong state, not new mistakes. The Medium paper's "-46.90 percent tokens" still has no source in our logs. **Ranked next tests:** (1) the confirmation test (built, item 90); (2) a retry once on an unreadable reply, with the parser's message (not built; the one measured hole; cost one extra call on refused steps); (3) a real model on 3,000 to 6,000 steps in state mode (**corrected 12 Oct: built as item 93 on a local open model, which needs no key; "a paid key or about a week" held for Gemini only**; the retry is not part of it); (4) a second kind of task, not graphs (the first objection a reviewer will raise); (5) chat histories of 100,000 to 1 million tokens built from the oracle's log (needs a paid key); (6) a second model family. Not run: any of 2 to 6.
93. **Million-token test on a real model: 3,600 steps of Qwen2.5-7B with the scope filter (12 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - the test item 92 named as still to be run, **built and pre-registered, not yet run**. A pasted suggestion to use free open-weight models on a Colab or Kaggle GPU instead of a paid key is right for this test: a model that runs inside the notebook has no quota. Its other suggestions were checked and not taken as written: Groq or Cerebras with Llama is a separate second-model-family test that needs a new backend (our API code is written for Gemini, so it is not "change the URL"); the "work around the Gemini quota" numbers (15 a minute, 1,500 a day) do not match this account (20 a day for 3.8 Flash, 480 for Flash Lite), `sleep(4)` does nothing for a daily cap, a second key in the same project shares the quota, and extra accounts to multiply a free quota are against the terms (not built). `MILLION_TOKEN_TEST.md` (hash-locked page: predictions with a confidence each, 13 reading rules, what each NO would mean; `experiments/million.py`; test 99 `test_million.py`, 37 tests, which also cover item 94; mutation check 176 of 179 caught, the 3 survivors are equivalent changes): Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in 4-bit on a free Colab T4, one episode of 3,600 requests (stream seed 20261021; about 1.2 to 1.5 million tokens by estimate, the real counts replace it), policy scope_m7, rule not stated, **no cache so every counted token is a token sent**, every step saved to disk so a dropped session resumes by replaying the saved replies with every saved flag checked; a contrast of blind apply, the gate and repair_m6 on the first 300 requests of the same stream; an optional second seed for "two million". The question it exists to answer: do slips that are legal, so invisible to the gate, pile up faster than the stream removes them (lines 6 to 9) over a real job. Overall chance that lines 1 to 12 all come out yes: 30 % (line 8, at least 80 % of the last tenth equal to the oracle, is 50 %). **Run it:** `python/colab/axi_million_test.ipynb` with `axi-colab-million.zip` (about 70 to 110 minutes for run 1, then about 20 for the contrast). The Result section and `test_million_result.py` (test 100) are added when the files come back. Not run; the history-kept side at this length stays an extension (the GPU runs out of memory near 5,500 tokens).

94. **The same million-token test on a current hosted model: Gemini 3.5 Flash Lite (12 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - the question after item 93, "why test it on a small model?". **Built and pre-registered, not run.** `MILLION_TOKEN_TEST_GEMINI.md` (hash-locked; the same stream, the same 13 reading rules and the same thresholds as item 93, so only the model changes, and with it three things: replies are sampled at the default temperature, there is no grammar, and a repeated prompt is answered from the saved reply and not counted as sent; thinking tokens count as sent). Overall chance that lines 1 to 12 all come out yes: 15 %; the guard and instrument lines 65 %. The expected weak point is line 8 (35 %): the 30-step Flash Lite runs read 120 of 120 exact for scope_m7, but unreadable replies appeared on deletes (3 of 317 with no rule stated, 75 of 334 with it stated, all the id-with-a-number slip), and a reply that cannot be read leaves the held state where it was, which the scope filter cannot see. The page therefore fixes beforehand that the cause of every new offset (unreadable reply or readable reply that did the wrong thing) is counted from the saved rows, and that if it is the unreadable replies the finding is that a re-ask step is needed (a separate test, **not built**; not claimed to fix it). Code: `million.py` gained thinking-token counts, `cached` rows that are not counted, a token cap per run (2,000,000), `smoke_report` and `estimate_cost`; `DiskCachedLLM.last_think_tokens`; `python/colab/axi_million_gemini.ipynb` with the same `axi-colab-million.zip` (free key: 480 calls a day, so about 8 days; paid key: about an hour; **the code never turns billing on and no price is built in**: a price list pasted from another assistant was not verified and is not used, the notebook prints a dollar figure only from two prices typed in from the AI Studio page). A 20-step check on a different stream decides one thing before run 1: thinking stays at the default unless it spends more than 20 thinking tokens a step. Qwen (item 93) stays the free control. The Result section and `test_million_gemini_result.py` are added when the files come back.

95. **The HARD million-token test: Gemini 3.8 Flash, 4,000 steps on a dense job with strict rules (13 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27)** - the user's request after item 94: "use 3.8 Flash and make the test as hard as you want; I am ready to pay; let's get something concrete out of it if it passes." **Built and pre-registered; run 1 and the contrast made on 13 and 14 Oct (result below); the control and run 2 are not made yet.** `MILLION_TOKEN_TEST_HARD.md` (hash-locked; the Result is appended below the locked text). Harder than items 93 and 94 in six ways: a **dense graph** (up to 24 services and 48 edges; 17 to 22 and 18 to 37 on average; a prompt is 1.5 times as long); a new request kind **`delete_hub`** (a service with 4 to 8 dependants, the rule about dependants not stated; 8 percent of the steps); a **re-ask** when the strict parser cannot read a reply (up to twice, every attempt billed and checked by the replay); the repair deleting **up to 8 dependants**; **14 strict reading rules** (every tenth at least 80 % exact, at least 90 % overall, a distance that never passes 4 items, at most 1 % of asks done wrongly, at most 0.5 % unreadable; the headline "PASSED" needs nine lines at once; the one sentence a pass allows is written in the page, and what it does not allow); and a **dollar guard**. Predictions: headline pass about **20 %** (line 6 is expected to fail first, 30 %), the guard and instrument lines 43 %, all fourteen lines under 3 %: the page expects a NOT PASSED and says what is then reported (slips per thousand steps by kind and cause, how long offsets last, what the re-ask bought through a control arm). **A finding made on the way, with a stand-in program and not a model:** the framework's repair (`degree_threshold=4`) KEEPS a deleted service that has more than 4 dependants instead of deleting it with them, so a model that forgets the dependants of a hub leaves a service the oracle has deleted, and the distance stays; the hard test sets the repair to 8 (written into the header of every file; the default is unchanged and the safe default is not claimed to be wrong). Code: `experiments/million_hard.py` (stream, 14 lines, headline, `registered`, reports), `million.py` (`stream=`, `reasks=`, `degree_threshold=`, `scripted=`, billing summed over every call of a step), `api_llm.py` (`BudgetStop`, the dollar guard: refuses the next call once the saved answers cost the budget at the typed-in prices, thinking as output), `cascade.next_state(degree_threshold=)`, `endurance.Stream.max_nodes/max_edges`; `python/colab/axi_million_hard.ipynb` (refuses to start without PAID, two typed prices and a budget; never turns billing on; a 20-call check after 200 steps the program answers itself, so the graph has grown; decides one thing, the thinking level, before run 1). Cost estimate on UNVERIFIED prices ($0.75 and $3.75 per million tokens, pasted from another assistant, said to double on 1 January 2027): about $4 for run 1 and about $10 with everything, if the model thinks about 100 tokens a step. Test 100 `test_million_hard.py`, 32 tests; mutation check 150 deliberate breaks tried; 126 were caught by the first version of the tests, 24 were not and showed 21 loose spots in the tests (edge cases such as exactly at the budget, the hub setting on a resumed run, two counters that could not be told apart), all tightened and now caught; the last 3 cannot change any result (a guard that can never fire, a second check of what an earlier check already ensures, a value that does not depend on the setting). Not part of it, and not built: the comparison with a chat that keeps its history.
    **RESULT OF RUN 1 AND THE CONTRAST (13 and 14 Oct; test 101 `test_million_hard_result.py`, 11 tests; `experiments/million_hard_result.py`; files `docs/results/axi_million_hard_run1_seed20261031_default.json`, `axi_million_hard_cache_after_run1.jsonl` and `axi_million_hard_contrast_seed20261031_default.json`): the headline is PASSED by the locked rules** (lines 1, 2, 3, 5 to 10 yes; 11, 12 and 13 yes; **line 4 NO**; line 14 not run). 4,000 steps, 3,661,209 tokens sent (2,926,781 prompt, 80,984 reply, 653,444 thinking), **every step exactly the oracle's state (4,000 of 4,000), distance 0, never corrupted**, the prompt 648.8 then 755.4 tokens from the first tenth to the last (1.16 times, following the graph and not the step), 2.57 then 2.41 seconds a step, two retries after a busy server, $4.95 at the typed-in prices (arithmetic, not a result). **What did the work in run 1:** no reply was refused (the re-ask was never used) and the scope filter removed nothing; the model's only slip was in "delete a service, and also remove this edge", where it left the service's dependants out in 46 of the 97 requests that had dependants (0 of 472 for the plain "delete the service" requests with 1 to 8 dependants), and the deterministic repair completed all 46. The setting of 8 dependants made no difference on this run (the dependants left out were at most 3). **The contrast (first 400 requests, same requests, each arm on its own held state; $0.69):** the framework 400 of 400 steps exactly right; the model's replies applied as written (blind) exactly right on 85 and corrupted (edges pointing at a deleted service) on 223 of 400 (55.8 percent, under line 4's bar of 80, hence NO), first at step 16, in four offsets of 36 to 150 steps, all after the same kind of request; a gate that only refuses never corrupted and exactly right on 84, off the oracle's state on every step from 210 to 400 (mean distance 5.5 in the last hundred); repair_m6 400 of 400, a replay of run 1's replies (the scope filter made no difference on this stream). Line 13 yes (0.00 against 1.83 and 4.63 in steps 201 to 400). **Cost per step with the right state (added after the contrast was read; arithmetic on the saved rows, not a reading rule):** with every call counted at the typed-in prices the three arms spent about the same (332,838, 333,040 and 347,263 tokens; $0.47, $0.45 and $0.48), so a step with the right state cost $0.00117 with the framework, $0.00524 (4.5 times as much) applying the replies as written and $0.00573 (4.9 times) with the gate; tokens and dollars only, no energy figure, nothing on what a wrong state costs afterwards, no comparison with a chat that keeps its history. Predictions: lines 6, 7 and 8 (rated 30, 40, 45 percent) all held, line 13 (45 percent) held, line 4 (55 percent) did not, and the 20 percent for a headline pass happened; the page expected a NOT PASSED. **Still open:** run 2 (line 14), the control (it will find every prompt already saved and show the same, since no reply was refused). **Not measured:** a chat that keeps its history; an agent that reads its state back and corrects it; a Pro model; another provider; a task whose state grows; the blind arm over 4,000 steps. Mutation check of the result test: 922 deliberate changes to the Result's numbers and sentences, to the module and to the three saved files, 915 caught by the test and 7 not; those 7 change nothing that the page reports or that these files can tell apart, namely two comparisons of item sets that never differ here, a strip of spaces that no reply has, a thinking count of exactly 1,000, the seconds of the contrast arms, the token count of reused replies in the contrast table, and the rule that names how a stretch of corrupted steps ended when a stretch can only end one way.

96. **Keep or drop the interior tokens in the KV cache, on a stock model (14 Oct; rows 3, 9, 27; items 89 and 92)** - the user's request, after two forwarded AI explanations (one on five "tensor-level mechanisms", one proposing this test): "Yes, let's run it. Build the pre-registered page and the notebook for Qwen on Colab. Let's compare keeping versus dropping interior tokens in the KV cache, and measure exactness, cache tokens saved, and time per step with the predictions locked first." **Built and pre-registered; NOT RUN: no number from a model exists.** `KV_DROP_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 0e13a782b7c530a739df955cb6047f2882fe7b7e3c6c39f013a2bc8aef186c43 of the text; the Result goes below it), code `experiments/kv_drop.py` (all the logic, no torch) and `experiments/kv_torch.py` (the Hugging Face side), notebook `colab/axi_kv_drop.ipynb` (upload `axi-colab-kv.zip`), checks `tests/test_kv_drop.py` = **test 102** (22 tests) with `tests/kv_standin.py`, a small real transformer in numpy (rotary positions, grouped-query attention, a cache that carries the rotation of its rows). Inference time only: no model is trained. **Design:** one logical prompt per step; teacher forcing (the state is the oracle's, the arms never feed their own mistakes back); five arms: `fresh` (read again from nothing: the reference), `exact` (cache kept, request and reply cropped, the items after a delete read again: a prefix cache, so a saving is not just prefix caching), `drop_compact` (the deleted item cut out, nothing re-read, the keys behind the hole turned to the positions a fresh read would give: stale rows only), `drop_gap` (the same without the turn: stale rows and a position gap), `keep` (a chat with its cache reused). Run A: 8 episodes of 30 steps, no rule; run C the same scripts with the rule stated; run B: 2 episodes of 100 steps. Per step: reply identical to fresh's (token by token), KL along the reply, task exactness (blind and repair_m6), tokens read, tokens kept, bytes, peak memory, seconds split into update, prefill and decode, stale rows, position gap, value deviation. **15 reading rules with my probabilities** (90, 85, 80, 75, 90, 70, 80, 80, 90, 70, 75, 75, 65, 60, 60 %); headline (lines 2, 3, 5, 6, 14) about **26 %**, and the page says I would bet against all five. **Found before any run:** (1) in the first wording a line break followed the last item; by Qwen's pre-tokenizer rule a bracket plus a line break is one pre-token and would merge into one token, so line 1 would have read NO for a dull reason; the closing tag now follows a space, and a toy tokenizer with that rule shows both the problem and the fix (the real tokenizer is still checked by `preflight` and line 1); (2) the key check was first an absolute difference, which 16-bit keys of this model could fail by rounding alone; it is now relative to the size of the keys. **What the checks show, on the numpy model only:** the exact arm holds the cache of a fresh read (keys, values, positions in every layer) and writes the same reply with KL 0; the drop arms equal fresh until the first delete in the middle and differ after it (a random model, so this shows the instrument and nothing about Qwen); adds are exact; deleting the last items is exact; `keep` is exactly the chat and stops at its cap; a resumed run equals an uninterrupted one; every reading rule flips at its threshold; every code cell of the notebook runs on the stand-in and the notebook stops when a check fails; **preflight** (9 checks run on the real model before any run: pieces = one pass, keys turned by 211 positions = keys read at those positions in every layer and the check can tell, next-token logits, cut and re-read, select-all identity, a hole in the positions does not hide earlier rows, size reporting, tokenizer pieces) fails for each of five deliberately broken caches. **Mutation check:** 69 deliberate changes to the code, the page, the notebook, the PyTorch side and the stand-in; 64 were caught by the tests as first written and the other 5 (the memory cap of the chat arm to the token, the preflight line 'read in two pieces', the share of steps under 0.05 nats, a tie in reading rule 11, the rows over which the value deviation is taken) led to stricter tests, so that all 69 are caught by the final tests. **Not tested anywhere yet:** the PyTorch side against the installed Hugging Face version (no torch in this workspace); its tolerances (0.5 in logits, 0.05 relative in keys) are guesses for 16-bit numbers with 4-bit weights, so the first Colab run may stop at preflight and print its numbers. **Claims in the two forwarded explanations that this test does NOT support, whatever it finds:** "the compute requirement flattens to O(1) ... for months" (30 or 100 teacher-forced steps are measured, no more); "a microsecond pointer swap" and "seconds to milliseconds" (the test copies tensors with an index select and a rotation in Hugging Face; no paged or copy-on-write allocator is built or timed); "perfectly preserving" the other lines (for a stock model that is the approximation the test measures); "dropping 70 percent or more of the interior tokens proves compaction works on stock models" (no such target is set; the replies are 10 to 20 tokens, there is no reasoning scratchpad to drop, and this model's cache is about 57 KB per token, so a 500-token prompt is about 28 MB: memory is not the constraint on a T4 at these sizes); "nobody has published pre-registered numbers" (not checked beyond three papers: Prompt Cache, CacheBlend, Block-Attention). The other three mechanisms in the first explanation (orthogonal subspaces, Lie-group positions, a 1.2 microsecond mask) are not touched: the first two need training and are not built, and the mask cost measured here is 77 microseconds per token at batch 1 (item 89). **Next:** run it (about 1 hour, $0), then the Result section below the locked text; options after it: a second model family, an edit-in-the-middle arm, a repair that reads a small share of the stale rows again (CacheBlend-style). Not run: any of it.

97. **Keep or drop the interior tokens when the state is thousands of tokens long (14 Oct; items 96, 89 and 92; rows 3, 9)** - the user's request, after being told the first test uses graphs of a few hundred tokens and what bigger would cost: "Let's try thousands of token one a bigger graph. Or let's do A bigger model:14B". I read it as the bigger graph first (it runs free on the same T4 and is the more informative step) and the 14B as its own page later. **Built and pre-registered (14 Oct); RUN on 15 Oct: runs A and C made, run X not made; the headline FAILED on line 14 (RESULT at the end of this item).** `KV_LARGE_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 999e01007464fddb2de4aac7acd533353589d14f3f4e9587ea9ade1424695aef of the text; the Result is below it), code `experiments/kv_large.py` (no torch: the large graph, the request script, `dry_run`, the summary and the sixteen reading rules) on top of `kv_drop.py` (which gained one optional argument, `script_fn`, and behaves as before without it) and `kv_torch.py`, notebook `colab/axi_kv_large.ipynb` (upload `axi-colab-kv-large.zip`), checks `tests/test_kv_large.py` = **test 103** (13 tests) on the numpy stand-in of test 102. Inference time only: no model is trained. **Design:** the test of item 96 with a start graph of 120 services on a 14 x 14 grid and 200 edges (prompts of about 4,000 tokens; run X: 240 services, 400 edges, about 8,000), the same four request kinds, teacher forcing, four arms (`fresh`, `exact`, `drop_compact`, `drop_gap`; no `keep`, because one state is already thousands of tokens and a chat would pass 20,000 tokens within a few steps). Run A: 4 episodes of 40 steps, no rule; run C the same scripts with the rule stated; run X: 2 episodes of 30 steps on the bigger graph. **16 reading rules with my probabilities** (90, 85, 75, 75, 60, 55, 85, 50, 95, 90, 75, 90, 50, 55, 95, 45 %); headline (lines 5, 7, 8, 13, 14) product about **7 %**, and because the lines move together my single number for all five is about **25 %**; the page says I would still bet against it. **Found before any run:** (1) with the prompt layout of item 96 (services first, then edges) a delete sits in the first quarter of the prompt, so `exact` (a prefix cache) must read the whole edge list again and saves much less than at a few hundred tokens, while the drop arms keep more than nine rows in ten stale: that is what makes the large test a hard one for the cache, and it is written on the page as a property of the layout, not of the idea; (2) a **dry run without any model** (`kv_large.dry_run`, crude token counter, not Qwen's tokenizer) gives for run A: first prompt about 3,800 tokens, `drop_compact` reads 3.7 % of what `fresh` reads, `exact` 36.5 %, `exact` about 9.9 times `drop_compact`, 92.8 % of the state rows stale at the last step; for run X: about 7,800 tokens, 3.9 %, 37.6 %, 9.7 times, 93.7 %. The page says so and marks lines 1, 9, 10 and 15 as near-arithmetic that complete the table rather than risky claims; a test pins the page's table to the code's output, and another shows that `dry_run` reproduces EXACTLY the tokens the arms read and the stale share they report when the token counter is one token per byte (the stand-in's); (3) my first dry run counted the stale share without the header rows (98.8 %); the arms count them in the denominator (they are never stale), which gives 92.8 %, so the dry run was corrected before the page was written. **What the checks show, on the numpy model only:** the graph has the stated sizes in every seed, every request of 160 steps is valid on the oracle's graph, the mix of kinds and the caps hold, `pick_large_task` is `cascade.pick_task` with a bigger grid for add_node (same randomness used), the script hook changes nothing by default (same rows as before) and swaps the script when given, the preflight passes on the large script and fails for a broken cache, the file header carries the script's name and arms and another script is refused, a finished run is not run again, every reading rule flips exactly at its threshold, every code cell of the notebook runs on the stand-in and the notebook stops when a check fails. **Mutation check:** 82 deliberate changes to the code (the graph, the script, the dry run, the summary, the reading rules, the runner), the script hook in `kv_drop.py`, the page and the notebook; 78 were caught by the tests as first written, 2 changed a line that does the same job as another (the gate check of an add_node on a cell already known to be free; a `>=` against `>` where the item compared is itself in the excluded set), so they cannot be caught and are not counted, and 2 led to stricter tests (the first third of a summary taken one step short, which two episodes of a stand-in happened to hide, now has a hand-made-rows test; the length of the quick check, which only its printed title gave), so that all 80 changes that matter are caught by the final tests. **Not tested anywhere yet:** the PyTorch side at 4,000 and 8,000 tokens (no torch in this workspace); the tolerances of the preflight are guesses and a longer prompt makes rounding differences larger, so the first Colab run may stop at preflight and print its numbers; the time estimates (45 to 70 minutes per run on a free T4) are guesses, and the notebook measures the real machine and prints a projection before the long runs. **Not supported by this test, whatever it finds:** anything about a 14B model (its cache is about 197 KB per token, so four caches of 4,000 tokens next to 9 GB of weights are borderline on a free T4; it needs its own page and a paid GPU, a few dollars an hour), constant compute over months, microsecond pointer swaps. **Next (written 14 Oct, before the run):** run item 96's notebook first (a small, fast check that the PyTorch side works), then this one; then the Result sections; options after: the 14B page, a second model family, an edit-in-the-middle arm, a repair that reads a few percent of the stale rows again. **RESULT (15 Oct; runs A and C made on a free Colab T4, run X NOT made; test 106 `test_kv_large_result.py`, 8 tests, recomputes every figure from `docs/results/axi_large_{a,c}_Qwen2.5-7B_seed21.json`):** of the 16 lines 14 read yes, **line 14 reads NO** and line 16 is not run; **the headline FAILED on line 14 alone** (I had put the five lines together at about 25 %). **Run A (no rule, 160 steps per arm):** `exact`, `drop_compact` and `drop_gap` wrote exactly `fresh`'s reply on 160 of 160 steps with 93.5 % of the rows in the drop caches stale at the last step; `drop_compact` read 3.5 % of the tokens of a rebuild (21,221 against 609,270; `exact` 224,483, 10.6 times more than `drop_compact`) and took 1.50 s a step against 8.25 s (`exact` 2.81 s). But the reply in run A is a copy of the request (11 tokens on average), so a faithful cache and an unfaithful one would have looked alike; my 50 to 60 % on lines 5, 6, 8 and 13 was low for how easy that regime was. **Run C (rule stated):** `drop_compact` identical to `fresh` on 115 of 160 steps (71.9 %), `drop_gap` 70.0 %, `exact` 97.5 % (4 steps, KL 0.011 at most: the floor that rounding alone leaves); the 45 differing steps are 34 of the 52 deletes with dependants and 11 of the 19 deletes of a service without edges, none of the 89 adds; KL above 0.05 nats on 51 of 160 steps (largest 8.2). **But the rebuild is poor at the task itself:** on the 52 delete replies with dependants `fresh` lists 0.23 of the service's own edges (0.42 of those that start at it, **0.04 of those that end at it**), writes 2.85 edges per reply that are not in the graph, and 14 of the 52 replies run into the 120-token limit; `drop_compact` is at 0.24 / 0.41 / 0.04 and 2.83. Repaired exactness: `fresh` 82.5 %, `exact` 81.9 %, `drop_compact` 79.4 %, `drop_gap` 80.0 %; `drop_compact` gained 6 steps and lost 11 against `fresh` (exact sign test p = 0.33: not distinguishable from chance). **No reply in any arm listed an edge that had been deleted earlier (0 'ghosts').** Seen afterwards and NOT pre-registered: the agreement of `drop_compact` is 84.6 % over the first third of the steps and 67.9 % over the last (p = 0.046; on the delete steps alone 8 of 23 against 18 of 24 differ, p = 0.008), a reason to test whether it grows with the number of deletes, not a finding (4 episodes, steps not independent). **My reading:** cutting a deleted item out is invisible while the reply does not depend on that item and visible when it does; the replies that change are mostly one wrong edge list replaced by another; the saving in tokens (3.5 % of a rebuild) and in time (0.18 times) is real whatever the quality question's answer; and what the runs show most clearly is not about the cache: even a rebuilt prompt does not let this model find the edges that END at a service, which is what items 98 and 99 take up. **Not run:** run X (about 8,000 tokens), the small test of item 96, the 14B page.

98. **Does the order a graph is written in change what a stock model finds in it? A layout test on Qwen2.5-7B (15 Oct; items 96 and 97)** - the user's request, after sharing a long text on a "structural geometry language" at the level of a transformer's tensors and after telling me I had treated it as only material for the repo: "Test 2 section any of your choice you think you can test and make a difference from it". I took the two parts of it that a stock model lets one test without training (this item and item 99) and wrote on both pages that the rest (orthogonal subspaces, Lie-group position codes, gauge connections) needs a model trained for it and is not tested; I have not checked with Rajnish Choubey that the text's formulations are his and do not attribute them to him. **Built, pre-registered, RUN and read (15 Oct, free Colab T4, Qwen2.5-7B, 250 reads, 37.6 minutes). The headline FAILED on all three of its lines (5, 7, 8 read NO); of the 13 lines 7 read yes (1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12, 13) and 6 read NO (5, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11); my single number for the headline had been 35 %.** `LAYOUT_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 c9c3cd72018428d55338b2f350b3605a4fc50a7091319badbbbe439ed2f142cc of the text above the Result; the Result is appended below it), code `experiments/kv_layout.py` (no torch), notebook `colab/axi_tensor_tests.ipynb` (Cell 8; upload `axi-colab-tensor.zip`, shared with item 99), checks `tests/test_kv_layout.py` = **test 104** (16 tests) on the numpy stand-in of test 102 and, added after the run, `tests/test_kv_layout_result.py` = **test 111** (10 tests, no GPU: it recomputes every figure of the Result from the saved file `docs/results/axi_layout_Qwen2.5-7B_seed31.json`, scores all 250 replies again from their text and checks that the page above the Result is unchanged). Inference time only: no model is trained, no cache is kept, no episode: single reads. **Built on what Run C of item 97 showed:** in `fresh` the 52 delete replies find 0.42 of the edges that START at the service (next to each other in the usual layout) and 0.04 of those that END at it (scattered over the edge list), so the question is whether it is where the edges sit. **Design:** 14 graphs (70 services, 120 edges, 10 x 10 grid, prompts of about 2,300 tokens), 3 problems 'delete the service at (r,c)' per graph on services with 1 to 3 edges (5, 15 and 22 problems with 1, 2 and 3 edges; of their 101 edges 53 start and 48 end at the service) and, on 8 graphs, one control (add an edge or a service, alternately): 50 problems; five conditions of the same facts: `sorted` (the layout of every earlier test), `shuffled`, `grouped` (each service followed by the edges that start at it), `adjacency` (each service followed by ALL its edges, both directions, so every edge is written twice, with one added sentence in the system prompt; about 1.65 times the tokens), `rot90` (state and request turned a quarter turn: one of eight frames, a probe and not a test of 'rotation blindness'); the rule stated; 250 reads, seed 31, $0, 35 to 45 minutes (estimate). Per problem: recall of the service's own edges (split into those that start and those that end at it), invented edges, exactly right as written and after repair_m6, ran on to the limit; against `sorted` problem by problem with an exact sign test. **13 reading rules with my probabilities** (85, 85, 99, 85, 65, 92, 75, 55, 35, 70, 60, 80, 95 %); headline (lines 5, 7, 8) product about 27 %, my single number **35 %**. **Found before the run:** figures I had first written on the pages were wrong and were corrected before the lock (the split of the edges, 49 / 51 became 53 / 48; the size estimate; figures taken from the earlier run), and the line text in the code and on the page differed in one place (the code now follows the page). **Related work (the three papers exist as named: confirmed by a search on 15 Oct; what they report is from my memory of their abstracts, check before citing):** Lost in the Middle (Liu et al., 2023), Premise Order Matters in Reasoning with Large Language Models (Chen et al., ICML 2024), Talk like a Graph (Fatemi et al., ICLR 2024). **What the checks show, on the numpy model only:** the quarter turn is a bijection and four turns are the identity; each condition holds exactly the facts of the graph in the order the page names (`adjacency`: every edge twice, after each of its two services; `shuffled`: both lists shuffled, services still first); the turned problem is the same problem (state, request and ideal reply); the request builder equals `cascade.pick_task`'s text; the 50 problems are the ones the page describes (the fingerprint of the set is pinned); each condition is read with its own kit (only `adjacency` has the extra sentence); the scoring of a reply (recall in both directions, invented, other) on scripted replies, a reply cut off at the token limit counting for what it wrote; the 13 reading rules flip exactly at their thresholds; a finished run is not run again, a dropped session continues, a file made with other problems is refused; every code cell of the notebook runs on the stand-in and the notebook stops at a failed check. **Mutation check (items 98 and 99 together):** 195 deliberate changes to the code of both tests, the stand-in, the PyTorch side, the two pages and the notebook; 179 were caught by the tests as first written, 2 cannot be caught (the changed line does the same job as the original), and 14 led to stricter tests (the control parameters and the fingerprint of the problem set, the shuffled layout changing both lists, the fingerprint noticing every part of a problem, each condition read with its own kit, gain and loss against `sorted` not symmetric, the first block check reading the whole state as one block, a fixture for each of the two 'the check can tell' lines, the positions of the kept cache strictly increasing, `block16` meaning blocks of 16 items, the source of the PyTorch side (which cannot run here) held to what its review found, the adjacency token check kept in the notebook), so that all 193 changes that matter are caught by the final tests. **Mutation check of test 111:** 89 deliberate changes to the saved file, the page and the scoring code; 88 caught by it (one more test was added for the item-by-item reader after the first run showed two changes to it were not noticed by test 111), the one left (the order of the DEL items in the ideal reply, which no score reads) is caught by test 104. **Not tested anywhere yet:** the PyTorch side of the block test (`forward_blocks`, item 99); the layout run used the single-read path, and the notebook stops at a failed check before Cell 8. **Not supported by this test, whatever it finds:** anything about Lie-group, gauge or orthogonal-subspace position codes (they need training), rotation blindness in general, other models, other graph sizes. **What the run found (every figure recomputed from the saved rows by test 111; the page says which reads were NOT pre-registered):** recall of the service's own edges 0.333 `sorted`, 0.329 `shuffled`, 0.302 `grouped`, 0.373 `adjacency`, 0.440 `rot90`; in `sorted` the edges that start at the service are found at 0.534 and those that end at it at 0.103 (a gap of 0.431; Run C: 0.42 and 0.04). Exactly right as written (of 42): 9, 2, 8, 4 and 8; with `repair_m6`: 33, 19, 41, 35 and 26; replies cut off at the 120-token limit: 5, 21, 0, 7 and 11. **Line 5 NO** (scrambling lowers the recall by 0.004, the line needed 0.10: the model does not use the fact that a service's edges sit next to each other); **line 7 NO** (`adjacency` +0.040, needed +0.15); **line 8 NO** (`adjacency` exactly right as written 11.9 points WORSE than `sorted`, not 15 better; 3 problems better, 8 worse, p = 0.23); line 9 NO (`grouped` -0.032); line 10 NO (`rot90` +0.107, outside the 0.10 band by 0.007; the paired data cannot tell it from chance, p = 0.42); line 11 NO (`adjacency` invents 65 % more edges, not 30 % fewer); line 6 yes. The reading I had written for 5 NO and 6 yes: the model is order-insensitive in the large and direction-sensitive in the small; for 7 NO: the layout is not the lever for this model at this size and the premise of the text (that order is what hurts) is not supported for how many true edges are found. **Seen afterwards, NOT pre-registered (reasons to test again, not findings):** scrambling makes the replies not end (21 of 42 cut off) and with the repair step 19 are exactly right against 33 (3 better, 17 worse, p = 0.003, which survives a correction for the eight sign tests); `grouped` has no reply cut off and 41 of 42 exactly right with the repair step against 33 (8 better, 0 worse, p = 0.008, which does NOT survive that correction: 0.06), so it is a candidate for a replication; with the repair step the outcome follows whether the reply parses and whether it deleted an edge of another service (none of the 44 unparsed replies is repaired to the right state; each of the 12 replies that parsed and stayed wrong had deleted a real edge of another service); counting an edge as found when its arrow is written backwards, 13 of the 101 edges are written that way in `sorted` and 22 in `adjacency`; `adjacency` finds more of the edges that end at the service (0.391 against 0.103, +0.287, 14 problems better and 2 worse, p = 0.004) and fewer of those that start at it (-0.155), at 1.68 times the tokens, and its added sentence was never tested alone. **Not shown:** that any layout is better, anything about rotation in general (one frame of eight), other models or sizes, sampling, or the position codes of the text (they need training). **Next:** a pre-registered replication of `grouped` against `sorted` on a new seed with about 100 delete problems; a request that states which way the arrows run (tested on `sorted` and `adjacency`, and `sorted` with the added sentence); the block test (item 99) is still not run.

99. **Can a stock model read every item of its state in a block of its own? Block independence without training, on Qwen2.5-7B (15 Oct, run 16 Oct; items 96, 97 and 98)** - the second of the two tests chosen for the request of item 98. It takes section 2 of the shared text ("sheaf-factorized block attention", whose table lists "no pre-training required: applied as an attention mask at test time") and asks the one version of it a stock model allows: compute the key/value rows of every item on its own (the header in view, the other items not), put the rows side by side, and let the request read all of them. If the replies survive, a delete is exact for that model (no stale row) and an add costs only that item; if not, "no pre-training required" is false for this model and this task. The text says nothing about having measured it; I have not checked with Rajnish that the formulation is his and do not attribute it to him. **Built, pre-registered, RUN B1 and RUN B2 and read (16 Oct, free Colab T4, Qwen2.5-7B; B1: 3 episodes x 30 steps x 4 arms = 360 replies, 36.6 minutes, no rule in the prompt; B2: 3 x 20 x 4 = 240 replies, the rule stated). The headline FAILED on two of its four lines (5 and 6 read NO; 10 and 11 read yes); of the 11 lines run B1 can read, 8 read yes (1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11) and 3 read NO (5, 6, 7); run B2 (the rule stated) read lines 12, 13 and 14 NO, so over both runs 8 of the 14 lines read yes and 6 read NO; my single number for the headline had been 40 %.** `KV_BLOCK_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 8f893996d9df330886651ac4cecdd957da76d41a501e5a72e5bd7ecaa38d6b04 of the text above the Result; the Result is appended below it), code `experiments/kv_block.py` (no torch: reading rules, dry run, the seven checks for the real model) with `BlockArm` and `Tracked.forward_blocks` in `kv_drop.py` and `TorchCache.forward_blocks` in `kv_torch.py`, notebook `colab/axi_tensor_tests.ipynb` (Cells 9 and 10), checks `tests/test_kv_block.py` = **test 105** (12 tests) and, added after the run, `tests/test_kv_block_result.py` = **test 112** (14 tests, no GPU: it recomputes every figure of the Result from the two saved files `docs/results/axi_block_b1_Qwen2.5-7B_seed21.json` and `docs/results/axi_block_b2_Qwen2.5-7B_seed21.json`, scores all 360 and 240 replies again from their text, also through `kv_drop.score_reply`, and checks that the page above the Result is unchanged). **Design:** the prompts and request scripts of item 97 (120 services, 200 edges, about 3,800 tokens; the first steps of the same scripts, seed 21), teacher forced, four arms: `fresh`; `drop_compact` (item 97's cache, for comparison); `block` (every item its own block; the cache is kept between steps; a delete cuts the rows and turns NOTHING, because the deeper layers of a row were computed at its position, and turning the keys alone would not equal a rebuild; an add is read as a new block after the last row, leaving a hole in the positions); `block16` (blocks of 16 items, rebuilt every step, contiguous positions). B1: 3 episodes x 30 steps, no rule; B2 (optional): 3 x 20, rule stated. Every 10 steps the kept `block` cache is compared with one rebuilt from nothing at the same positions. **14 reading rules with my probabilities** (97, 95, 93, 95, 55, 65, 50, 65, 85, 95, 85, 40, 55, 40 %); headline (lines 5, 6, 10, 11) product about 29 %, my single number **40 %**, and the page says I would still bet slightly against it. The page says why I expect worse than item 97's run A: every row loses every other item here, in run C a smaller change moved two replies in three on the delete steps, and the three papers on independent chunks that I know (Prompt Cache, CacheBlend, Block-Attention) report a loss without training or recomputation. **Found before the run:** a dry run without a model (crude counter, not Qwen's tokenizer) says `block` reads 4.5 % of what `fresh` reads over B1 and 6.1 % over B2 (line 10 is this arithmetic, which is why it has 95 %); the first draft of the page had figures from the earlier runs and the share of delete steps wrong (the stale shares are 93 % and 92 %, the value differences 0.22 and 0.21, the delete steps 37 % of 71), corrected before the lock. **What the checks show, on the numpy model only:** `forward_blocks` reads each block with the header and itself and no other block, at the positions it is given; a block added later sees the header only; one block holding the whole state is a normal read; the request reads every block; the kept `block` cache equals a cache rebuilt from nothing at the same positions, and that comparison fails for a cache that closes the gap or lets a new block see the others; what the arms read is what the page says; the 14 reading rules flip exactly at their thresholds; the page's dry-run figures are the code's; both tests of the notebook run end to end on the stand-in, save after each run, never save outputs and stop at a failed check. **The PyTorch side (`TorchCache.forward_blocks`) could not be run in this workspace (torch cannot be installed here); it ran on the Colab T4 on 16 Oct (run B1, below).** Its test is `preflight_blocks`, seven lines on the real model (one block holding the whole state gives a normal read's logits; the rows of block B do not change when block A has other tokens, and that check can tell, because A's own rows do change; in a normal read B's rows DO change; a cut equals a build without A at the same positions; a block added afterwards equals the same block built with the others; the request reads every block), run in Cell 6 before any long cell; the notebook stops if one fails, and its tolerances (0.5 in logits, 0.05 relative in keys) are guesses. **Mutation check (items 98 and 99 together):** 195 deliberate changes to the code of both tests, the stand-in, the PyTorch side, the two pages and the notebook; 179 were caught by the tests as first written, 2 cannot be caught (the changed line does the same job as the original), and 14 led to stricter tests (the control parameters and the fingerprint of the problem set, the shuffled layout changing both lists, the fingerprint noticing every part of a problem, each condition read with its own kit, gain and loss against `sorted` not symmetric, the first block check reading the whole state as one block, a fixture for each of the two 'the check can tell' lines, the positions of the kept cache strictly increasing, `block16` meaning blocks of 16 items, the source of the PyTorch side (which cannot run here) held to what its review found, the adjacency token check kept in the notebook), so that all 193 changes that matter are caught by the final tests. **Not tested:** a mask inside the attention kernel, training, the text's 'interface' (a block that exposes only a few tokens), blocks that follow the graph, other models. **What the run found (every figure recomputed from the saved rows by test 112; the page says which reads were NOT pre-registered):** `block` (every item its own block) wrote the same reply as `fresh` on 43 of 90 steps (47.8 %), and all 43 are adds (22 of 28 add-edge, 21 of 22 add-service);
