on all 40 deletes (31 of a service with dependants, 9 of a leaf) it wrote a different reply and the repair step turned none of them into the right state (47.8 % right after `repair_m6` against 100 % for `fresh`); largest KL below 0.5 nats on 40.0 % of the steps (largest 5.325). `block16` (blocks of 16, rebuilt every step) was identical on 86 of 90 (95.6 %; the 4 that differ are deletes with dependants). `drop_compact` (item 97's cache) was identical on 90 of 90 (largest KL 0.005). The mechanism held: the kept `block` cache equals a rebuild at the same positions exactly (0.0 in all 9 comparisons), `block` read 14,827 tokens against `fresh`'s 346,483 (4.28 %; the dry run said 4.5 %) and took 2.81 s a step against 8.64 s (0.325 times; 1.79 s against 8.62 s after the first build, which took 32 s). The value rows differ from a normal read by 0.936 (mean relative L2) against 0.134 for `drop_compact`. **Line 5 NO** (47.8 % against 90 %), **line 6 NO** (52.2 points below `fresh` after the repair step; 3 allowed), **line 7 NO** (40.0 % against 90 %); lines 1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11 yes. **Run B2 (16 Oct, a few hours after B1; the rule stated in the system prompt; 3 episodes x 20 steps x 4 arms = 240 replies, 60 steps per arm; the counter said 2,303 s; saved in `docs/results/axi_block_b2_Qwen2.5-7B_seed21.json`; I had written that I would not spend the time on it before the block-size run, and it was made anyway):** **lines 12, 13 and 14 all read NO**; over both runs 8 of the 14 lines read yes (1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 9, 10, 11) and 6 read NO (5, 6, 7, 12, 13, 14). `block` was identical to `fresh` on 28 of 60 steps (46.7 %; line 14 needed 70 %), right after the repair step on 75.0 % against 83.3 % for `fresh` (8.3 points below; line 12 allowed 5) and right as written on 51.7 % against 61.7 % (10.0 points below; line 13 allowed 5); KL below 0.5 on 45.0 %, largest 9.777. Lines 12 and 13 miss by margins the paired data cannot tell from chance (exact sign tests p = 0.38 and 0.11); line 14 does not. The mechanism held again (the kept cache equals a rebuild exactly in 6 checks; `block` read 233.3 tokens a step against 3,963.6, 5.89 %, in 4.02 s against 11.89 s, 0.338 times). Where the loss is: on the 21 deletes with dependants `block` finds none of the service's own edges and names 28 invented ones, but after the repair step it is right on 14 of 21, the same as `fresh` (whose own lists are mostly wrong too), so the repair hides the delete loss here; the loss is on the adds (add-service right on 12 of 17 against 17 of 17; the five that differ write an edge item with the service's coordinates at both ends, the same flip of the second token as in B1). With the rule stated the flip is less total: 9 of the 26 delete replies of `block` open in the edge form, 17 in the service form (B1: 40 of 40). **Blocks of 16 are the worst arm in B2, not the best:** identical on 33.3 % and right after the repair step on 61.7 % (5 steps better and 18 worse than `fresh`, p = 0.011, about 0.06 after correcting for the six paired tests); 14 of its 17 add-service replies write the service and then go on to write `DEL[...]` items for edges of the new service (0 of 17 for the other three arms) and 22 of its 60 replies run to the 120-token limit. So what the page said after B1, that bigger blocks lose the same thing at a much lower rate, holds without the rule only (95.6 % in B1) and the page now says so; no block size tested here is shown to keep the replies in both prompts. `drop_compact` kept 76.7 % identical and 83.3 % right after the repair step, the same as `fresh`, as in Run C of the large test. My reading of the outcomes ('independent blocks work where the reply is a copy and fail where it has to search') was written for 5 and 6 yes and does not apply; what the two runs show together is that single-item blocks change the replies in both regimes (47.8 % and 46.7 % identical). **Seen afterwards, NOT pre-registered:** the six looks of the 'What I read from the rows afterwards' list of the B2 section of the page. The headline (5, 6, 10, 11) failed on two of its four lines; my product was 29 % and my single number 40 %. My list of outcomes had no entry for 5 NO, 6 NO, 8 yes; the nearest ('5 NO and 6 yes with 8 yes') said that a stock model needs some cross-item context and that the next test is the block size; for the claim as the shared text states it, line 5 NO is the answer for this model and this task: 'no pre-training required' is false for independent single-item blocks here. **Seen afterwards, NOT pre-registered (reasons to test again, not findings):** the failure is one decision: in all 40 delete replies the second token is `[(` (an edge) where `fresh` writes `[` (a service), after which `block` names an edge that is not in the graph (31 invented edges on the 31 deletes with dependants, none of the service's own); the replies already differ at step 1 of every episode, before any hole in the position numbers exists (gap 0), so independence alone is enough; the fall from 56.7 % to 40.0 % between the first and the last third is the mix of requests (on adds 17 of 20, 14 of 14 and 12 of 16; on deletes 0 in every third); three of `block16`'s 4 differing replies have the same shape; `block` is worse than `drop_compact` on identical replies, on the repaired state, on KL and on time, equal on tokens read, and better on stale rows (none, against 93.8 %) and peak memory (6.9 GB against 10.4 GB); I do not know why the second token flips. **Mutation check of test 112:** 204 deliberate changes to the two saved files, the page and the code the test reads; the first version, written for B1, let 5 of the first 134 through (two quoted figures that also stand elsewhere on the page, a second Result heading, the scoring function `kv_drop.score_reply` itself, and one change aimed at a step already above the line); after B2 was added 3 more got through (two page quotes that were too loose and one `drop_compact` reply text that no figure used); the test was tightened each time and all 204 are caught. **Not seen by me:** the output of the seven `preflight_blocks` checks on the real model. **Not shown:** a trained or fine-tuned model (the related work says it is what the design needs), block sizes between 1 and 16, blocks that follow the graph, a kept cache of blocks of 16, other models or sizes, why the second token flips, the text's 'interface'. **Next:** the block size (1, 2, 4, 8, 16) on the same scripts in both prompts (size 16 was nearly right without the rule and the worst arm with it), with a pre-registered line first; blocks that follow the graph and a kept `block16`; a look at which rows the request reads at the `[` or `[(` decision, and at why blocks of 16 append `DEL` items to an add when the rule is stated.


100. **Can many agents write one shared graph at the same time? A concurrency test of the independence rule, the gate and the copy-on-write pager (15 Oct; the second of the three ideas in the text the owner pasted that day)** - the text says that, because history is irrelevant, an agent is stateless, so "10,000 AI agents can read and write to the exact same enterprise state graph simultaneously using Copy-on-Write (CoW) shadow paging", that "Without AXI, multi-agent swarms inevitably overwrite each other's context and corrupt the shared environment" and that "AXI acts as the atomic transaction manager for the swarm". It gives no test. The owner objected that I had weighed the text by whether the repository already had it: a thing the repository lacks is a new thing and is to be tested on its merits. The page says what the repository does hold (the gate judges one edit against one state; the pager is a copy-on-write page table; `Runtime.propose` handles one proposal at a time; nothing exercised two proposals open on the same snapshot) and then sets that aside. **Built, pre-registered, RUN and read (15 Oct); all 19 lines read yes, including the headline (lines 5, 6, 10, 16).** `SWARM_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 e18d1a644f9b3394e8845df20f40c28a2cf86a4c6eeb7a7d94771a87656b2a04 of the text above the Result; my probability for the four headline lines together was 70 %), code `experiments/swarm.py` and, added after the result, `experiments/swarm_exhaustive.py`, checks `tests/test_swarm.py` = **test 107** (17 tests), `tests/test_swarm_result.py` = **test 109** (6 tests, recomputes every figure of the Result from the saved files) and `tests/test_swarm_exhaustive.py` = **test 110** (5 tests). CPU only, no model, no internet: `python -m axi.experiments.swarm --out axi_swarm.json` (about two minutes; the digest of the pairs, rounds and sweep is **dd08fd515ff59bff** under Python 3.11 and 3.13). **The rule tested** is the textbook parallel independence of double-pushout rewriting (Ehrig, Pfender and Schneider 1973; Corradini et al. 1997), fitted to the gate's edits with one added clause because the gate names its items: two edits made on the same snapshot are independent when what both MATCH is only what both KEEP and neither creates what the other creates or matches. Three detectors (`dpo` the rule, `pager` the real `Pager` as `Runtime.propose` uses it, `names` = the items written overlap) were judged against the truth: apply both orders through the real gate; they commute when both are accepted and give the same graph. **Result:** over 240,000 pairs (20 graphs of 40 services and 60 edges per regime, uniform and hot, seed 41), 4,842 do not commute; **`dpo` missed 0 and raised 0 false alarms** (in both regimes); `pager` **missed 1,320 of the 4,842 (27.3 %)** and in the hot regime raised false alarms on 2.47 % of the pairs that commute (and 1.36 % in the uniform regime, which I had not expected: two agents who each attach an edge to the same service collide on its page); `names` missed 40.0 %. In rounds of 16 agents on one snapshot (10 arrival orders): `merge` ended in one graph in 100 of 100 rounds in both regimes, first come first served (`gated`) in different graphs in 83 of 100 hot rounds, and the ungated `blind` store left an edge with a missing end in 52 of 100 hot rounds (68 uniform); `gated` and `merge` take the same share of the edits (0.874 and 0.875). The share kept by `merge` depends on agents divided by services: 99.8 % (4 agents on 640 services), 96.2 % (64 on 640), 87.2 % (64 on 160), **69.9 % (64 on 40)**; the nine cells lie on one curve. Eight real threads on one store, 300 rounds, 2,400 attempts: with the rule 2,083 accepted and 317 refused as clashes, the gate never refused what the rule passed, the log replayed through the gate gives the final state; with the gate alone 2,035 accepted and 365 refused, the same four properties; the last-writer control store lost updates (2,400 of 2,400 "accepted", replay different). **Exhaustive pass (added afterwards, NOT pre-registered):** three cells, the 80 graphs on them, every gate-accepted edit of one to three operations, every ordered pair: 1,262,688 pairs, 1,075,824 not commuting; `dpo` 0 missed, 0 false alarms; `pager` 51,397 missed (4.8 %) and 137,912 false alarms; `names` 61,976 missed (5.8 %). This is exactness on three services and edits of at most three operations, not a proof for the language. **My reading, derived afterwards:** (1) the rule is exact for this edit language on these random edits and on the small universe; (2) `merge` does not keep more edits than first come first served, it makes the outcome independent of the order of arrival; (3) **with one head, the gate alone already keeps the state correct** (line 17); what the rule adds is a deterministic batch, a check that needs only the two edits and not the current state (a replica or shard could run it) and a measure of how much a crowd can do at once; it adds no safety to a gate that sees the head; (4) the Python `Pager` as written is wrong in both directions as a judge of what may be taken together, so "copy-on-write shadow paging" as the text states it is not what makes the swarm safe here (a statement about the Python pager and `Runtime.propose`, not about the Rust crate); (5) every one of the 4,842 clashes was a pair the gate refuses in both orders, so the clauses about what one edit creates and the other needs never mattered in these runs. **All 19 lines came out as I wrote them and I do not take that as a sign that the test is strong:** I wrote the instrument, the truth and the predictions; the rule is a textbook one; lines 8 and 11 passed by 0.47 points and 3 rounds. Where my hand numbers were off (the page asked for this): the clash share (I said 2 to 2.5 %, it is 1.97 and 2.06 %), the spread of `names` between regimes (47.4 % and 32.9 %), the false alarms of `pager` in the uniform regime (none expected, 1.36 % found), the clash share in the threaded runs (8 % expected, 13.2 %), the ill-formed rounds of the blind store (10 % expected, 52 to 68 %, and fewer in the hot regime than in the uniform one, which I cannot explain from these data). **Mutation check (swarm):** 96 deliberate changes to `swarm.py`; 34 survived the tests as first written; 28 of those led to stricter tests (the hand-checked choice of hot services, a scripted pair run with an invalid edit, a one-order clash and a failed first commit, the digest, the threaded accounting and others), 6 cannot be caught (the changed line does the same job: 28, 40, 57, 75, 76, 80), so 90 are caught by the final tests. **Does not show:** 10,000 agents (the largest round has 64; the threads are 8; the curve above is a fit of nine cells, not a measurement beyond them); that an LLM agent needs only the current state; what an agent proposes (the edits are random); atomicity under hardware races or on several machines (eight Python threads under one interpreter lock); the Rust crate; the "O(1) pointer swap"; an edit that updates in place (the language has none; the rule would need a read-write clause); a second, independently written implementation of the truth and the rule (mine are written by one hand). **Next:** a second implementation of the truth by another hand; an in-place update kind; more services in the exhaustive pass; processes instead of threads. The question of the owner (is the pasted statement Rajnish Choubey's) is still open and the page does not attribute it.

101. **Does the way a DENSE graph is written change what a stock model can read? A representation test of the packed bit-matrix idea (15 Oct; the first of the three ideas in the pasted text)** - the text says that the framework "explicitly defines a relational density threshold" rho = |E| / (|V| (|V| - 1)), that at or below 0.65 it "streams coordinate tuples to the model" and above it "text serialization causes catastrophic token bloat and attention confusion", so it "dynamically cuts over to Packed Bit-Matrix Representations (64-bit integer bitsets)", which it calls "a massive, unmeasured efficiency lever". It gives no measurement. **What the repository holds (looked up, stated on the page so that nobody has to trust the word "missing"):** `crates/axi-ir/src/router.rs` has a `DensityRouter` with the default threshold 0.65 that returns `SparseTuples` or `PackedBitMatrix` (two unit tests, no caller); its density is (services + edges) over the cells of the grid, which is not the formula of the text (by it the sparse graphs of this test would be routed to the packed form too); nothing turns the label into text for a model. `python/axi/wire.py` is a binary frame with a coordinate form and a bit-matrix form, and `python/tests/test_wire.py` pins its size crossover at 1/128 of the cells, not 0.65: a question about bytes on a wire. **What a model can read of either form has not been measured in the repository.** This test asks that. **Built and pre-registered (15 Oct); NOT RUN: no number from a model exists.** `DENSE_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 e5a2b70b7ddaaae3b2473238a291045b9431a8f182134e9f866e6e6b3e387059 of the text; the Result goes below it), code `experiments/kv_dense.py` (no torch), its own Colab notebook `colab/axi_dense_test.ipynb` (Cells 1 to 8; upload `axi-colab-tensor.zip`, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct 4-bit on the free T4; Cell 6 is a safety check without model time, Cell 7 a quick look that also times the machine, Cell 8 the run; each finished (graph, format) pair is saved to Drive), checks `tests/test_kv_dense.py` = **test 108** (22 tests, on the numpy stand-in, with the notebook run cell by cell on stubs). **Design:** 24 services on a 5 x 5 grid; 5 dense graphs (400 edges, rho 0.725) and 3 sparse (138 edges, rho 0.25), seed 51; each written in five ways (`tuples`: the repository's own text; `adjacency`: per service the services it points to and those that point to it; `matrix`: 24 numbers 0 or 1 per row; `binary`: the same rows as unbroken strings; `bitset`: each row as one 64-bit integer in hexadecimal, the text's format); 8 questions per graph, the same in every format (2 out-neighbour lists, 2 in-neighbour lists, 4 existence questions); free greedy decoding, no grammar, at most 160 new tokens, every question a fresh prompt; 64 questions per format. A decoder reads every prompt's state back to exactly the graph's edges. Because a dense graph makes a lazy strategy strong, each list question has a **baseline**: the F1 of answering with all the other services (0.83 on the dense graphs, 0.36 on the sparse ones). **What the design alone fixes (a lower bound of Qwen's tokens by its pre-tokenizer, computed before the run):** dense: tuples 4,982, adjacency 4,248, matrix 1,459, binary 918, bitset 743 (0.29, 0.18 and 0.15 of the tuples for the three matrix forms); sparse: 1,838, 1,628, 1,459, 918, 789. So by tokens the packed form is shorter than tuples from about 60 to 70 edges, a density of about 0.11, not 0.65 (as in bytes on the wire). The text is right that tuples for a dense graph are big; whether a model can USE the packed forms is what the run asks. **14 reading rules with my probabilities** (99, 99, 80, 90, 90, 85, 75, 72, 80, 80, 60, 65, 75, 80 %); headline (lines 4, 5, 9, 11: the packed forms save most of the tokens and a stock model reads them at about chance and worse than the plain text) product about 39 %, my single number **45 %**. I expect the saving and expect the reading to fail; the page says what each outcome would mean, including that a failure is not a finding against the bitset on the host side. **Found before the run:** my first draft of the page said I had not found the cut-over in the repository; I then found `router.rs`, corrected the page and told the owner; the token arithmetic of the first draft was also corrected before the lock. **What the checks show, on the numpy model only:** each format is what the page says on a hand-made three-service graph (bit order included) and is read back to exactly the graph's edges by its own decoder, and a tampered state or a wrong column order is not; the token count is the pre-token bound on hand cases and the page's table is the code's; the scoring (parse, precision, recall, F1, the asked service not counted, the baseline) is right on hand cases; greedy decoding stops at the end token, is cut at the limit and stays in the vocabulary; every question is a fresh prompt holding the state and the question and nothing of the truth; the runner writes one line per pair, resumes after a dropped session and refuses a file made with other settings; the 14 rules flip exactly at their thresholds and say "not run" when numbers are missing; the page is locked; the facts the page states about `router.rs` and `wire.py` are checked against the files; the notebook runs cell by cell, holds no key and stops at a failed safety check. **Mutation check:** 105 deliberate changes to `kv_dense.py`; 89 were caught by the tests as first written, 9 survived and led to stricter tests (which services a list question may ask about, the fingerprint of the graphs and of the question texts, the state's token count by the tokenizer, where decoding starts, the whole reply kept, the summary's "decodes" flag and two of the checks that the same questions were answered in every format), 7 could not be applied as first written and were redone; 0 cannot be caught; all are caught by the final tests. **Not tested here, whatever it finds:** the host side (a bitset is a normal structure in Python or Rust), a model trained to read bitsets, the cut-over at exactly 0.65 (two regimes only), writing edits (only reading), other models, other graph sizes (a row of the text's packing holds 64 services at most). Related work from memory, not searched: Fatemi, Halcrow and Perozzi, Talk like a graph (2023); Wang et al., NLGraph (NeurIPS 2023); I do not remember either testing a packed-integer form. **Next:** run Cells 1 to 8 of `axi_dense_test.ipynb` (about 25 to 40 minutes, my estimate; Cell 7 measures it), send back `axi_dense.json`, then the Result below the locked text. Not run: any of it.

102. **What does the host layer do when the proposer is not a language model and the world changes by itself? A stale-snapshot test (17 Oct; the first of the four tests of an AI-written roadmap the owner pasted that day)** - the roadmap (no author, no sources; I have not checked with Rajnish that any of it is his, so none of it is attributed to him) asks for a test in which a small trained neural controller works on a grid whose edges and nodes change "without the agent asking" with a chance s per step, and says it would validate "Topos Theory and Heyting Logic under sensor noise" and show whether "Attractor Manifolds" can "snap" a noisy, out-of-sync proposal into the nearest safe action. The repository has no code for topoi, Heyting algebras, attractors, Voronoi cells or spinors (searched), so **the test says nothing for or against that mathematics**; it measures the discrete host layer: the gate, and the repair `diagnostics.ground_and_complete` (the nearest thing in the code to a "snap"). (The first version of the page said that this item already existed as planned; it did not, the idea had been planned in the conversation only; the Result of the page says so.) **Built, pre-registered, RUN and read (17 Oct); 14 of 15 lines read yes, line 8 reads NO (1.70 against 2); both headlines read yes** (A: the exact reader, lines 4 to 7, my probability 75 %; B: the trained network, lines 12 to 15, 65 %). **The 14 yes are not a sign of a strong test: lines 5 and 6 are close to true by construction (the repair carries the same reading of "delete a service" as the definition of a right step; the retry reads again on the current state), and I wrote the instrument, the oracle, the route check and the predictions.** `DYNAMIC_WORLD_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 1a824d8b7d243a2f72a28c63ce4491a96d20c13914834258e4919ceeed3d8619 of the text above the Result), code `experiments/dynworld.py`, checks `tests/test_dynworld.py` = **test 113** (22 tests) and `tests/test_dynworld_result.py` = **test 114** (7 tests, recomputes every figure of the Result from `docs/results/axi_dynworld_seed20261101.json` and, in its heavy part, replays the world). The small network is the file `docs/results/axi_dynworld_net.npz` (sha256 3426ca055461781f394222c00f727b0d0b9bac269645fef1681c6762c6f4c744). CPU only, numpy, no model, no internet: `python -m axi.experiments.dynworld --out axi_dynworld.json` (about six minutes on two CPUs; the digest of the requests, the changes and the oracle's states is **0011b2ff1ab98427**). **Design:** a 5 x 6 grid of services with directed weighted edges; 1,000 requests an episode, 20 episodes a cell, 10 cells (regime `uniform` or `near`, the change of the world landing next to what the request names; chance of a change s = 0, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4 a step). The proposer reads a SNAPSHOT of the held state taken before the change, so its answer is one change old when the host handles it. Proposers: `exact` (the meaning of the request on the snapshot: isolates the age of the snapshot) and `net` (a numpy network of 1,027 inputs, 128 hidden units, 930 x 3 outputs, trained on clean streams; right on 77 % of clean replies). Arms: `blind` (apply as written), `gate`, `retry` (the gate; if it refuses, ask again on the current state) and `repair` (`repair_m6`). A right step is the request's meaning on the arm's CURRENT state (my definition, stated on the page). **Result:** with the exact reader at s = 0.4 near, blind apply slips on 6.68 % of steps and leaves an edge to a missing service in its state after 82.1 % of them; the gate slips on 3.75 %, never ill-formed, but ends 12.02 items from the real state (blind 3.98) and 38.9 % of the routes planned on its map are unsafe (blind 18.6 %): a refusal leaves the map behind. The repair and the retry slip 0 times in 200,000 steps; the retry costs 1.095 calls a step, the repair 1.000. With the network, every arm except the repair is wrong on about half the steps at every s (44 to 50 %); the repair on 8 to 11 %, with its held map 3.5 to 7 items from the real one (blind 49 to 98, the gate and the retry 66 to 108) and 9.5 to 13 % of the planned routes unsafe (blind and the others 76 to 84 %). **The changing world is almost invisible next to the network's own mistakes**, and I did not expect the size: I had estimated 23 % wrong steps (measured on the oracle's own state) and got 49.6 % in the closed loop, where each arm reads its own state. The repair keeps the map near the real one but not on it: it completes a cascade, it cannot know which edge was meant. **Does not test:** a robot, a camera or any sensor, real noise (the host is told every change at once, a trusted channel), continuous state or energy landscapes, a language model under a changing world, snapshots more than one change old, a second independent oracle (mine and the route check are one hand). **Next:** the version where the host is NOT told about the changes; a network trained on its own closed-loop states; a language model as the proposer under a changing world. The other three tests of the roadmap are items 103 to 105.

103. **Can a small transformer trained on graphs of 10 services answer about graphs of 100? A toy-model test of the roadmap's spinor arm (17 Oct; the second of the four tests of the AI-written roadmap)** - the roadmap (no author, no sources; I have not checked with Rajnish that any of it is his, so none of it is attributed to him) says: train "two identical small transformers from scratch to predict graph mutations", Arm A with standard 1D rotary position embeddings, Arm B with "Lie Group Spinor Embeddings (exp(sum omega T) in SU(2))" and a loss "enforcing Subspace Orthogonality"; train on N = 10, ask about N = 100; "Arm A will catastrophically fail ... Arm B will mathematically succeed because Lie group geodesics are scale-invariant". It gives no task, no recipe for either arm and no projectors, and wants 10 million parameters. **Built, pre-registered and RUN (17 Oct, the owner's Colab CPUs, one seed per run): 9 of 14 lines yes; lines 6, 7, 8, 9 and 14 NO; of the headline lines 5 yes, 6 and 7 NO, so the roadmap's claim did not hold in this toy (the page had said what that combination means: the failure at 100 comes from the construction of my SU(2) turn, and the test cannot tell whether a different SU(2) code would do better).** `TOY_MODEL_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 a5b7cfd346c1f47da8c80718fb9bbe2b5b2e8adbdf6496bd3f55d1863af37165 of the text above the Result), code `experiments/toymodel.py`, checks `tests/test_toymodel.py` = **test 117** (17 tests) and `tests/test_toymodel_result.py` = **test 120** (5 tests; recomputes every figure of the Result from `docs/results/axi_toymodel.json`, sha256 45114a99...; mutation check: 744 deliberate changes (every number of the Result, 23 sentences of it, 5 passages of the locked head, 300 numbers of the saved file, 6 settings of the code), all caught), notebook `python/colab/axi_toy_model.ipynb`. CPU only, numpy, no language model: a transformer of about 101,000 parameters (not 10 million), forward and backward pass written in numpy and checked against finite differences. **Design (every choice mine, not Rajnish's):** the task is "which tokens of the graph are hit when the service at cell c is deleted" (the label of a token depends on the query token and on that token alone, so it is a matching of coordinates; 10 times the coordinate range and 12 times the tokens at N = 100); five arms: `A_rope1d` (the roadmap's Arm A: rotation by the index in the sequence, coordinates as learned tables of 100 rows, only rows 0 to 9 ever trained, so **it fails at N = 100 almost by construction**), `A_rope2d` (a stronger standard baseline: axial rotary on the cell itself, relative, no tables), `B_su2` (my reading of Arm B: blocks of C^2 turned by exp(theta (x T1 + y T2)), non-abelian, so **not** translation-equivariant, which the instrument test shows; with the penalty on the blocks of W_Q and W_K that carry payload into the position columns and structure into the payload columns), `B_su2_free` and `A_rope2d_orth` (controls). Evaluation sets N10, N20, N40, N70, N100, `range100_n10` (10 services on a 100 x 100 grid: range without length) and two payload-shift sets; 3 seeds, 4,000 steps. **14 reading rules** with my probabilities; headline (lines 5, 6, 7: Arm A loses more than half at N = 100, Arm B keeps four fifths, B beats A by 0.20) **10 %**; my chance for "Arm B keeps what it learned" alone 12 %, for the axial baseline 35 %. **What I looked at before locking:** gradients against finite differences, the SU(2) closed form against the matrix exponential, orthogonality of every turn, and one development run (seed 1, 2,000 steps, in-distribution graphs of N = 10 that are in no evaluation set: exact 1.00 for `A_rope2d`, 0.54 for `A_rope1d`, 0.94 for `B_su2`), after which I changed the training length from 3,000 to 4,000 and nothing else; no model was ever scored on a graph larger than 10 services. **Does not test:** 10 million parameters, language, the "Grassmannian manifold", principal components of attention (the off-block share of the weights and the sensitivity of layer 1 to the payload are proxies), any other reading of the roadmap's spinor. **Result:** at N = 100 (exact accuracy, mean of three seeds) the roadmap's Arm A gets 0.8 % (by construction, as the page said), my SU(2) reading of Arm B 2.3 % (1.5 / 4.0 / 1.5 by seed), and the stronger standard baseline I added, an axial 2D rotary embedding, 76.8 % (61.5 / 89.5 / 79.5; line 8 reads NO by 3.2 points, the bar being 80 %, and with other seeds it could have read yes). The SU(2) arm already loses about half at N = 20 (51.5 % against 99.6 %) and gets 20.7 % on ten services with coordinates up to 99 (the axial baseline 100.0 %), as expected of a turn that is not translation-equivariant; this is the turn I built, not every spinor code. The penalty empties the penalised blocks (0.0002 % of the squared weight against about 50 %) and does not help the size; added to the axial baseline it lowers N = 100 in all three seeds (76.8 % to 58.7 %). Two lines read yes against my odds: line 3 (every arm learns the task at N = 10, 30 %) and line 12 (the penalty helps with unfamiliar payload ids: +18.3 points on the mean, but -5.5 in seed 1, 25 %); line 11 (less sensitivity of the first layer to the payload, 70 %) read yes on the mean but not in seed 3, and the lowest sensitivity belongs to Arm A, which has no penalty. Of the 14 lines, 12 read on the side I favoured. Not shown: any other SU(2) code (a translation-equivariant one in particular), 10 million parameters, a real graph-editing task, a mix of sizes in training, more than three seeds. The other tests of the roadmap are items 102, 104 and 105.

104. **Does snapping a hidden vector to the nearest allowed token in a hyperbolic ball do anything that masking does not? A hyperbolic-snap test (17 Oct; the third of the four tests of the AI-written roadmap)** - the roadmap's section 3 asks to take the final hidden vectors of an open model, project them into a Euclidean space with a softmax and into the Poincare ball with a "hyperbolic Voronoi metric snapping", and says the softmax "yields a >= 5% syntax failure rate" while the hyperbolic functor "mathematically guarantees a 0.00% syntax failure rate". It gives no model, no task, no prototypes, no map into the ball, no curvature. **The page says before the run what the sentence amounts to: any rule that picks among the tokens the grammar allows cannot write a refused token, in a flat or a curved space and with plain masking as well; the 0.00 % is true by construction and the ball does not "trap" anything.** The only thing the geometry can change is which allowed token is chosen, and that is what is measured. **Built, pre-registered and RUN (17 Oct, the owner's free Colab T4): 7 of 13 lines yes; lines 6, 7, 8, 9, 11 and 12 NO; of the headline lines 5 yes and 8 NO, so the roadmap's claim did not hold (the page had said what that combination means: the restriction does the work and the ball adds nothing over masking).** `HYPERBOLIC_SNAP_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 5201de5e7abfd889bea7babecd4337b73cc5628d645b77ca607873d140cd2d88 of the text above the Result), code `experiments/hypsnap.py`, checks `tests/test_hypsnap.py` = **test 118** (14 tests) and `tests/test_hypsnap_result.py` = **test 119** (5 tests; recomputes every figure of the Result from `docs/results/axi_hypsnap.json`, sha256 3010c7a1...; mutation check: 750 deliberate changes (every number of the Result, 15 sentences of it, 5 passages of the locked head, 300 numbers of the saved file, 5 settings of the code), all caught), notebook `python/colab/axi_hyperbolic_snap.ipynb` (two safety checks before the long collection). **Design (every choice mine, not Rajnish's):** `Qwen/Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct` reads the repository's prompt (`full`) or the graph and request only (`bare`) and the canonical reply, teacher forced, for 400 tasks; at every position the last hidden vector (before and after the final norm), the model's own pick, the allowed set of the repository's grammar table and the logits of the allowed tokens are stored; then, on the CPU, nine arms: `free` (the model's own pick), `masked`, `euclid_nn`, `euclid_tuned` (one scale), `cosine_nn`, `hyper_nn` (exponential map at the origin, one curvature from a grid), and three heads trained on half the replies (`lin_dot`, `lin_eu`, `lin_hyp`, 32 numbers, analytic gradients checked against finite differences). **13 reading rules** with my probabilities; headline (lines 5 and 8: the model's own pick breaks the syntax in at least 5 % of replies even bare, and the hyperbolic snap is within 2 points of masking) **8 %**; line 8 alone 10 %, because the nearest row of the output head is not what the model computes. **What I looked at before locking:** the stage 2 on made-up data only (planted signal, no-signal case, gradients); **I did not run the model** and have not seen a hidden vector of it. **Does not test:** free generation (teacher forcing: after a slip a real run would go elsewhere), a larger model, sampling, "Double Pushout rules", "Dirichlet-Voronoi cells", a model trained for the ball. **Result:** the model's own pick is refused in 400 of 400 bare replies (9.8 % of positions) and in 63 of 400 replies of the full prompt (0.6 % of positions). The hyperbolic snap picks the canonical token at 83.3 % (full) against 93.8 % for plain masking (74.2 % against 86.7 % bare), and below a tuned flat rule (92.4 %) and cosine (92.5 %); every restricted arm is 100 % allowed, ball or no ball. The three trained heads reach 94.6 to 95.5 % (full; the hyperbolic one is 0.7 and 0.9 points ahead, under the 1-point bar) and in bare the plain dot head is best (94.5 % against 92.8 %); a trained head is not something AXI does. The tuned scale and the curvature chose the largest value of their grids, so a wider grid is a labelled follow-up. Stage 2 took 44 minutes, not a few, because the grammar allows 32,837 tokens somewhere. The first hardware run found one bug (`unembed` kept a gradient), fixed. The other tests of the roadmap are items 102, 103 and 105.

105. **Does a pair of Betti numbers of the recent history carry what the last 50 commands carry? A history-shape test (17 Oct; the fourth of the four tests of the AI-written roadmap)** - the roadmap's section 4 gives Arm A "a sliding window of the last 50 text commands" and Arm B "only the current state and the Betti numbers (beta0, beta1) representing the topological cobordism of its recent history" over 10,000 steps, and says that if B matches A "you have proven that chronological intelligence is a geometric property". It gives no task, no complex, no accuracy. Nothing in the repository builds a cobordism (searched); **what is tested is two integers of the graph of the cells visited in the last 50 steps against the 50 commands, nothing more.** **Built, pre-registered, RUN and read (17 Oct); 10 of 13 lines read yes; lines 7, 8 and 13, the three of the headline, read NO. The roadmap's claim did not hold in this world, and the outcome is the one I had put at about 99 %.** `HISTORY_SHAPE_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 df14475473c1e221543d4806fbda5add7e1150b8cfe211b3ed80cf1f611e13af of the text above the Result), code `experiments/histshape.py`, checks `tests/test_histshape.py` = **test 115** (10 tests) and `tests/test_histshape_result.py` = **test 116** (6 tests; recomputes every figure of the Result from `docs/results/axi_histshape.json`, sha256 ebfa27391bddc3706fb5c1f7f29586c38e12c989075713d6a135b7bd8e8e1f17, replays the 60 walks against the digest, and with `AXI_HEAVY=1` runs seed 1 again: it reproduced the saved accuracies; mutation check: 517 deliberate changes (every number of the Result, 7 sentences of it, 5 passages of the locked head, 150 numbers of the saved file), all caught), notebook `python/colab/axi_history_shape.ipynb` (a second run on another machine, with a comparison to mine). CPU only, numpy and scikit-learn, no model: `python -m axi.experiments.histshape --out axi_histshape.json` (977 s on two CPUs; digest of the 60 walks **c30005542a6567da**). **Design (every choice mine, not Rajnish's):** an agent walks a 7 x 7 grid for 10,000 steps under a hidden mode (go round a loop, keep the direction, move at random); at every step a gradient-boosted tree model, the same settings for every view and not tuned, names the mode (MODE) and the next command (NEXT) from one summary of the past: `B_betti` (beta0, beta1 of the last 50 moves; beta0 is 1 on every window, so it is one integer), `B_betti3` (beta1 at three scales), controls (`state` the cell alone, `C_counts`, `C_hist`, `D_short` the last three commands) and the window readers `A_window`, `A_eng`, `A_all`, `A_match`; 3 seeds, 14 + 6 walks each, test walks unseen. **Result (means of 3 seeds):** MODE `B_betti` 68.6 %, `A_window` 93.9 %, the cell alone 36.4 %, majority 30.3 %; TOUR F1 0.698 against 0.940; NEXT `B_betti` 28.6 %, `A_window` 65.4 %, the cell alone 28.1 %: **on the next command the pair adds nothing to the cell**. Mean beta1 by mode: random 3.16, tour 1.41, drift 1.00. Three scales of beta1 reach 77.7 % on MODE (still 16 points below the window); the five command counts do worse (56.3 %) than the pair; the last three commands alone name the next command 57.1 % of the time, 87 % of what the window gets; the 26 repeat numbers of the window (`A_eng`) name the mode 82.9 % and the next command 28.9 %, so what the pair loses on NEXT is not special to topology: any summary that forgets which command was which loses it. **Does not test:** a language model reading a history, token cost, other complexes, a learned (not chosen) summary, a world where loops are what the task needs, the words "cobordism" and "chronological intelligence". **Reproduced** on the owner's free Colab CPU (17 Oct; `docs/results/axi_histshape_colab.json`): same digest of the 60 walks, every number identical, 12.3 minutes. **Next:** a learned two-number summary of the window as the fair comparison; other scales; a second world. The other tests of the roadmap are items 102 to 104.

106. **What does the GPU spend on a job when many jobs share it and the engine caches prefixes? A provider-side serving test (17 Oct)** - the owner's point that the saving is wanted by the company that runs the model, not by the customer. For that company the 87.1 percent fewer tokens of a plain loop (`ENDURANCE_TEST.md`, part A) is not the cost, because a serving engine caches the prefix of a kept chat; "a kept chat with the cache on" was named on that page as the fair competitor and was not measured. **Run in part (17 and 18 Oct, the owner's free Colab T4, vLLM 0.31.0): six of the eight cells ran; `KC` at 64 jobs (the free GPU time ran out inside it) and the optional `KR` did not.** `PROVIDER_SIDE_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 d37805796784b1a2c1925e7d764c017e313fa9fdfefeb4027217c70510e9a614 of the text above the Result; the Result is written below it), code `experiments/provider_side.py`, checks `tests/test_provider_side.py` = **test 122** (28 tests) and `tests/test_provider_side_result.py` = **test 123** (7 tests; recomputes every figure of the Result from the saved files `docs/results/axi_provider_side.json` and `docs/results/provider_side_cells/`; mutation check: 1,160 deliberate changes to the Result, the locked text, the saved files and the thresholds, all caught), notebook `python/colab/axi_provider_side.ipynb`. **Design (every choice mine, not Rajnish's):** Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (AWQ) on vLLM, the cache fixed at 80,000 tokens, the 30-step jobs of the long-horizon test, K = 8, 32 and 64 jobs side by side in lockstep, exact-state against a kept chat with the cache on, a kept chat with the cache emptied each round as a reference, a random-order variant; measured: GPU seconds per step, prompt tokens computed, the share of the prompt served from the cache. 13 reading lines, headline lines 10 and 12. **What I expect, written first:** at light load a kept chat whose cache works costs about as much as exact-state (the 87 percent does not carry over to a serving stack); with the jobs outnumbering the cache the cached share collapses and the kept chat takes many times the time (a model of a cache that I wrote gives 13 to 19 times; it is a model of my assumptions, not a measurement). **What came out (the Result is on the page):** 8 of the 13 lines read yes; lines 3 and 4 (the arms hold equally exact states) read NO; lines 2, 11 and 12 are "not run", because they need the 64-job kept chat; the headline pair is half read (line 10 yes, line 12 not run) and I do not call it either way. **Because lines 3 and 4 read NO, the page's own rule applies: the arms' times are not compared, and this run makes no claim that exact-state is faster than a kept chat.** Measured: the kept chat's prefix cache worked at 8 jobs (94.3 % of its prompt came from the cache, 0.617 seconds a step) and gave way at 32 (6.3 % in the second half, 5.149 seconds a step, 8.3 times as long); the exact-state prompt stayed at 516 tokens or fewer and 64 jobs of it need at most 41 % of the 80,000-token cache. Exact-state held the right state on 69.2 to 78.2 % of its steps against 97.3 and 100.0 % for the kept chat; no state was corrupted; what failed was replies that the strict parser refused, mostly on delete steps. The cause is not found: the replies that were not constrained are the most likely one, and that is untested. Where my expectation was off: lines 3 and 4 (85 % each) came out NO, and at light load the kept chat took 4.00 times the time of exact-state per step, not about the same (lines 9 and 10, which I put at 50 and 40 %, came out yes). Update 57 also puts the `fileno` fix into the notebook (vLLM 0.31.0 stopped in Colab without it) and pins vLLM to 0.31.0. **Not shown:** the kept chat at 64 jobs, a random order, another GPU, model, engine or cache size, energy (never measured), dollars, retries, replies constrained by a grammar. **Next, in this order, if wanted:** the same cells with constrained replies for all arms (a new page with new predictions), `KC` at 64 jobs on the same vLLM version, retries counted, the GPU's power read.

107. **The serving test again, with every reply constrained by the grammar, a quality-adjusted headline and a long cell that can be carried over Colab sessions (18 Oct)** - the owner's decision after the Result of item 106. A pasted AI text then promised that the constraint would "restore the exactness to 99 percent" and "formally lock in the 43 times speedup and the 8 times capacity"; checked against the saved files, not accepted: the 42.7 was a ratio of seconds per step at 32 jobs measured with the arms unequally exact (a page's own rule then forbids claiming it), the 8.3 was the kept chat's slowdown from 8 to 32 jobs and not a capacity multiple, and the six saved cell files say that exact-state will land near 88 to 96 percent and not 99, because 2 of the first 8 jobs (and 5 of 32, 6 of 64) missed in the first run on a reply that was well formed and wrong or on an add written like an edge, which a grammar cannot fix; what it should fix is the slip behind 84 of the 89 refused replies, `DEL[r,c:v]`. **Run (18 and 19 Oct, the owner's free Colab T4, vLLM 0.31.0): all six cells ran, `KC` at 64 jobs over three sessions.** `PROVIDER_SIDE_CONSTRAINED_TEST.md` (hash-locked; sha256 4559fdc4eda01013d456c1407c0bd23e3e634751394d6f65cc90d891cca7e575 of the text above the Result), code `experiments/provider_side_constrained.py` (it imports `provider_side.py` and does not edit it: that file's sha256 is pinned in the first run's merged result), checks `tests/test_provider_side_constrained.py` = **test 124** (20 tests: the regular expression against the repository's grammar and parser on random and mutated strings; the first run's refused replies recounted from its files; the adapter against a fake `vllm`; the cell runner and its checkpoint, stopped after every round and resumed; the 17 lines at their edges; the notebook, including the safety cell and a session that dies and is resumed; the page's figures recomputed from the saved files) and `tests/test_provider_side_constrained_result.py` = **test 125** (8 tests; recomputes every figure of the Result from the saved files `docs/results/axi_provider_constrained.json` and `docs/results/provider_side_constrained_cells/`; mutation check: 868 deliberate changes to the Result, the locked text, the saved files, the code and the Result's own sentences: 864 caught; the other 4 change the `distance` of a step that was already wrong, a number the Result does not use), notebook `python/colab/axi_provider_side_constrained.ipynb` (pins vLLM 0.31.0, has the `fileno` fix, runs a probe on the exact prompt of a reply the first run lost). **Design (every choice mine, not Rajnish's):** the first run's model, engine, cache (80,000 tokens), jobs and seeds, and both arms constrained by one regular expression that accepts exactly the delta language of the strict parser; cells `S` and `KC` at 8, 32 and 64 jobs, cheap ones first; 17 reading lines; **headline: exact steps per GPU hour at 8 and at 64 jobs** (a wrong step counts as nothing, for both arms); the plain time ratios are claimed as ratios at equal exactness only if line 5 reads yes. **What I expect, written first:** the constraint removes the format slips, `S` ends at about 88, 94 and 96 percent exact (lines 5 and 6, equal exactness and 98 percent, at 5 and 2 percent), the kept chat at about 100, and exact-state still does several times (8 jobs) to tens of times (32 and 64 jobs) the exact steps per GPU hour; the kept chat at 64 jobs is expected to take three to five hours, so the run needs two or three sessions. **What came out (the Result is on the page):** 14 of the 17 lines read yes. Lines 5 and 6 read NO as expected (5 and 2 %), and **line 2 reads NO, which I did not expect (94 %)**: one reply of exact-state (job 19, step 12, the same reply in the 32- and the 64-job cell) ran to the 96-token limit inside a name, because the regular expression leaves the length of a name open and greedy decoding looped; the strict parser refused it. **The page's own rule says that with line 2 NO neither the plain time ratios nor the exact-steps-per-hour figures are claimed, and the Result keeps to it: it reports the figures and claims neither.** Reported, with the exactness of both arms beside each figure: exact-state did 20,878, 28,748 and 32,896 exact steps per GPU hour at 8, 32 and 64 jobs (88.3, 94.6 and 97.2 % of its steps exact) against 5,923, 727 and 667 for the kept chat (100.0 % each), 3.52, 39.5 and 49.3 times; the headline lines 15 and 17 read yes, and counting the one cut-off reply as exact, or all 19 steps of that job, does not change a yes (checked afterwards, not pre-registered). The constraint removed the format slips (refused replies of exact-state 0, 1 and 1 against 6, 16 and 58 in the first run; jobs exact to the end 6 of 8, 27 of 32 and 58 of 64 against 3, 18 and 27); what is left is wrong content, and the jobs that missed are exactly the six the page named, at the steps it gave. The kept chat was 100.0 % exact in all three cells; its cache served 86 % of its prompt in round 6 at 64 jobs and gave way from round 7 (4.6 % in the second half), it took 5.395 seconds a step against exact-state's 0.106, and the 64-job cell took 2.87 hours of GPU (the guess was 3 to 5). **Not shown:** that exact-state is as exact as the kept chat (it is not: lines 5 and 6 NO), any claim from this run (line 2), another GPU, model, engine or cache size, dollars, energy (never measured), retries, a grammar that bounds the length of a name. **Next, in this order, if wanted:** the three exact-state cells again with the length of a name bounded (a new page with new predictions, a few minutes of GPU), retries counted, the same cells on another GPU, the GPU's power read.
108. **The exact-state cells again with the length of a name capped at 16 characters, to clear line 2 of item 107 (19 Oct)** - the owner's decision after the Result of item 107 ("Yes, build the re-run with the limited name length as Update 60 ... run those exact-state cells again to clear Line 2"). A pasted AI text had called the figures "undeniable proof", "49 GPUs with 1 GPU", "multi-million-dollar ... mathematically proven" and "4,900%", and said that putting constrained decoding back on "will close the gap"; checked against the Result, not accepted: constrained decoding was already on in item 107 and the gap that is left is wrong content (job 3 at step 16, job 4 at step 18, job 19 at step 12 and so on), not format; "49 GPUs", dollars and "proven" were never measured; the setup was one free T4, a 7B 4-bit model, an 80,000-token cache and 30-step jobs; 49.3 is a ratio of exact steps per GPU hour (97.2 % exact against 100.0 %), not a multiple of capacity, and the page forbids that wording. **This test could clear line 2 and nothing else. it does not obviously help the three shape slips (jobs 3, 19 and 101) and does nothing for the two extra deletes that the gate admits (jobs 17 and 62), so it cannot give 100 %, and it may make the exactness worse; it changes the prompt of every step, so every first reply may change. **Plan, not yet written on a page:** four cells of 64 jobs with the retry off, the old prompt and the anchor prompt, on jobs 0 to 63 and on jobs 128 to 191 (which no run has seen), in one session, about 16 minutes of GPU; the reading lines and the predictions are to be locked on a page before any run, and the owner says before anything is built. **Not accepted from the pasted blueprint:** that the conditional entropy of the output given the anchor is about 0 and is "the exact mathematical proof" (a story about attention, not measured); the example slip `ADD[(1,0)->(4,5):dep#7]` for `ADD[(0,0)->(4,5):dep#7]` (job 4, step 24 asked for exactly that edge and the model was right; the real slip is step 18); that the model repeated the wrong coordinate 21 times (14 of the 17 second requests asked after a slip were on the oracle's own reply); its classes and calls (`CICO_STRICT`, `verify_pushout`, `StateCollisionAbort`), which do not exist in the repository; "0.11 s per step" (wall seconds divided by the steps of 64 jobs side by side, not the delay of one request); and that the typed names (Durga, Laxmi) put the model in a subspace (they are words in the prompt; whether they help is also only a test).

On the 64 new jobs the capped expression gave 95.5 % exact and the grammar by kind 95.6 % (1,833 and 1,835 of 1,920 steps; 0.117 and 0.116 seconds per step; 29,437 and 29,560 exact steps per GPU hour, +0.4 %, the size of the spread between cells that did the same work): **the Result does not claim that the grammar by kind raises the exactness.** Four of the 5 jobs that slipped in `F0` slipped on a Delete (two named the wrong service, two wrote an edge and left the service out), which that grammar leaves free; the eight earlier first misses were all Adds. The grammar that names the coordinates (`FN`) and the target line at the top and the end (`FKA`) each had all 1,920 steps exact. **`FN` did it at 0.117 seconds per step and 30,882 exact steps per GPU hour, but the host wrote the coordinates it had read from the sentence, and the model wrote the value or the weight and, in each of the 681 deletes, the node alone (the framework's repair completes the edges): it does not show that the model reads requests.** `FKA` did it with the model choosing the node, at +53 % seconds per step, 20,191 exact steps per GPU hour and 5.4 per 1,000 tokens, with four jobs changed from missing to exact and none the other way (one run, 64 jobs); the line at the end only (`FKT`) did not (95.8 %). On the seen jobs `SK` has 1,906 exact steps (the ceiling) and `SN` 1,920, as the replay rule said. The parity check flagged all 14 first misses of the seven cells and no other step. The probe printout (Cell 6, sent the same evening and saved as `axi_provider_typed_probe.txt`) agrees with the saved runs on all 8 steps, and of the page's twelve side predictions 10 read true and 2 false: the grammar by kind wrote the oracle's reply at the 5 shape slips and at none of the 3 number slips, `named` and the target line at the top or at the end at all 8 (all 8 are Adds), the parity turn at 1 of 8. **What it tests:** the host reads the request sentence in Python (three forms; any other is an error, no fallback) and gives each request its own regular expression: `kind` (an add-service request may only be answered by one `ADD[x,y:v]`, an add-edge request by one `ADD[(a,b)->(c,d):name#w]`, a delete by `DEL` items only) and `named` (the kind and the coordinates and the relation the request names; a delete begins with `DEL` of the named service and may only delete edges that touch it). Seven cells of 64 jobs, arm `S`, no retry anywhere: `F0` the third run's capped expression (the control), `FK` kind, `FKA` kind with the target line (`TARGET (0,0) ADD NODE value 3`, item 110) at the top and the end of the user message, `FKT` the same line at the end only (it keeps the cache), `FN` named, all on jobs 128 to 191, which no earlier run has seen; `SK` and `SN` on jobs 0 to 63. A probe asks the eight known first misses under five variants and once with a parity turn (a user turn the host writes from the target and the wrong reply; a probe, never a step of the loop). 17 reading lines with a probability each, written first; the headline is lines 6 and 17 (exactness and exact steps per GPU hour of `FK` against `F0` on the new jobs; both yes about 35 %; lines 2, 3 and 4 all yes about 70 %; all 17 yes under 1 %). **What was read in the saved files before the page was written:** of the eight first misses in jobs 0 to 127 five have a shape the kind grammar removes (jobs 3, 17, 19, 62, 101; 66 steps) and three have the right shape and a wrong number (jobs 4, 20, 122; 23 steps), which only the named grammar removes, and there the model's share is the number of a node, the weight of an edge and the edge list of a delete; the oracle's own reply is inside all three languages for all 192 jobs (17,280 requests); with greedy decoding the third run's replies are replayed through the languages: in `SK` 1,860 steps are compared and jobs 4 and 20 must slip again at steps 18 and 30, in `SN` 1,848 are compared, and the ceilings are 99.3 % and 100.0 % against 97.2 %; the parity check flags all eight first misses and no step of the 5,040 saved steps of the four cells without a retry at which the job held the right state afterwards; a target line is about 12 tokens, and the top placement loses the cache beyond the system prompt (about 181 computed tokens a step against 109 on the cache counts of the third run). **Q = O / M** (from the pasted summaries of Rajnish's sessions, second-hand): `q_measure.py` computes, from the five saved runs, O (exact steps) over M as the wall seconds (every round) and as the tokens computed and written, shown as two numbers side by side, exact steps per GPU hour and per 1,000 tokens; no weight between a second and a token is invented, no floor of M is claimed; the table is on `Q_FROM_SAVED_FILES.md` and is recomputed by test 131. **What it cannot do:** it promises no 100 %: the kind grammar does not touch the three number slips, the named grammar copies the request into the reply, a job put right could slip again at a step nobody has seen, requests in free words are a different test, nothing is trained. **Read in the pasted texts and not built here, with the reason, each judged by itself:** `simulate_eval_suite` in one blueprint draws random numbers and prints them as accuracies, so nothing is taken from its output; a "5.4 times" figure divides the 64-job exact-state cell by the 8-job kept-chat cell (two different numbers of jobs side by side); "Q grows without limit" has no support, since every exact step needs at least its own tokens; writing in a missing precondition (a node a delete expects) puts a fact into the state that nobody stated, so the state could no longer be called exact; the weight-level items (an orthogonality loss, spinor rotary positions, a sheaf attention mask, a Poincare snap, Laplacian positional encodings, a drift module with a PyTorch layer) change or train tensors, which cannot be tested on a stock model served by vLLM, the setup this repository measures, so they stay roadmap; the attractor `relax` as written computes a primal closure; a hub patch changes nothing in a serving cell (no deleted service of the 192 scripts has more than 3 connections; the threshold stays 4 and `diagnostics.py` stays pinned). **Credit:** the typed operators, the anchored target and the quality ratio are Rajnish's ideas as the summaries state them (his own pages are needed for the exact wording); 114. **The serving side on a bigger model, counted the way a company that runs the model counts: drift, speed, latency, tokens and energy (21 Oct)** - the owner asked, after the seventh run's Result, for the big-model test he had suggested, on an L4 GPU or a v6e-1 TPU, measuring drift, speed, latency and efficiency (tokens or energy), and said AXI is for the companies that run models. **Status: built and checked here, then run once on Colab (21 Oct 2026); the page `PROVIDER_SIDE_BIG_TEST.md` was hash-locked before the run (sha256 fc73b30ce3cafc00... of the text above the line at its end) and its Result is on the page (test 138).** The code `provider_side_big.py` (it imports `provider_side.py`, `provider_side_typed.py`, `provider_side_long.py`, `provider_side_free.py`, `grounding.py` and `word_tasks.py` and edits none of them), the notebook `axi_provider_side_big.ipynb` and test 137 exist. **What it tests:** seven cells, no retry anywhere, on `Qwen/Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct-AWQ` (twice the size of the 7B), vLLM 0.31.0, one NVIDIA L4, a cache of 32,000 tokens, on the sixth run's jobs 192 to 223 (32 jobs) and 192 to 195 (4 jobs), 100 steps each: `P0` and `PN` (the exact state with the capped expression and with the grammar that names the coordinates), `P4` and `PC` (the exact state and the whole chat kept, on 4 jobs), and `X0`, `XW`, `XR` (the seventh run's controls repeated on the bigger model: the request with coordinates plus the names list, in ordinary words with the model reading the name, in ordinary words with the host reader). What it adds to the earlier engine: a record of request times where the engine reports them, and a GPU power meter (`nvidia-smi` every half second) around every cell, so that the Result can state seconds per step, computed prompt tokens per step, exact steps per GPU hour and the board's joules per exact step. 21 reading lines with a probability each, written first; the headline is lines 8 and 18 (the named grammar keeps the state; the bigger model reads the names itself; both yes about 22 %; lines 2 to 7 all yes about 72 %; all 21 yes under 1 %). A pre-flight check that can fail runs before the engine is loaded (16 checks, including that the GPU power can be read and the GPU is a free L4). **Why an L4 and not the v6e-1 TPU:** Colab's L4 rate is about 1.71 units an hour (a run of 2 to 3.5 hours costs about 4 to 6 units); the vLLM TPU project lists Llama, Gemma and Qwen3 but not Qwen2.5 text models, lists AWQ for v5 and v6 as untested, and the per-request grammar path has not been shown there, so the TPU needs its own probe and page and is not run. **What it cannot do:** it promises no 100 %; one model, one GPU, one run; the energy is the board's, not the host's; the 7B figures it is read beside are from a Tesla T4 in other sessions; the sentences are those of the seventh page and not the words of a user. **Run (21 Oct 2026):** the owner ran the seven cells on a Colab L4 in 41.0 minutes of generation (16,578 steps, vLLM 0.31.0), saved as `docs/results/axi_provider_big.json`. **12 of 21 lines read yes, 9 read NO** (9, 11, 13, 14, 15, 17, 18, 19, 20); lines 1 to 7 read yes, so the figures are claimed as read. Headline: line 8 yes, **line 18 NO** (the page put both yes at about 22 %). `PN` (named expression) held 3,200 of 3,200 steps at 0.110 s per step, 97 computed prompt tokens per step, 32,750 exact steps per GPU hour and 7.89 joules of board energy per exact step, flat over the job (last quarter 0.88 times the first); `P0` (capped) held 3,038 (94.9 %), below the 7B model's 3,075 on the same jobs, and `X0` (names list) 2,689 (84.0 %) where the 7B held 3,200. In all 78 first slips the reply had an item the oracle's reply does not have: the 14B does more than it is asked, which the named expression forbids. In words `XW` held 47.8 % (7B: 42.0 %), `XR` with the host reader 90.1 % (7B: 63.4 %); the reader read 2,688 right, 0 wrong, refused 512; all 17 first slips of `XR` are on refused requests. The kept chat stopped at steps 27, 61, 50 and 40 and was 1.81 times slower per exact step, 1.45 times growth, 1.24 times the computed tokens and 1.82 times the joules of the exact state on the same four jobs (all four below the page's thresholds). The board ran at about its rated 72 W in every cell, so energy followed time; this vLLM reported no per-request times, so no request-level latency is claimed. The L4 board figures are the board's, not the host's. **Credit:** the architecture is Rajnish's; the cells, the code and every measurement are the owner's, with an AI assistant as a tool. **Checked here:** the pinned files unchanged, the settings, the 7B figures on the same jobs read from the saved files of the sixth and seventh runs, the seven cells on a test double that obeys the grammar, the engine against a fake `vllm` module, the power meter's arithmetic and a real meter on a command, the 21 lines each at its edge, a cell not run reading "not run", the pre-flight check with each check made to read NO, the locked page, the notebook run on the double, these two front pages (test 137, 13 tests), and for the Result every step replayed from the text of its reply, the 21 lines by code and by hand, the figures recounted, the claims rule and these front pages (test 138, 7 tests); mutation check of test 137: not yet run; mutation check of test 138: not yet run. **Not shown:** anything about another model, GPU, TPU or engine, about the words of a user, about the host's or the building's energy, or about tasks of days; nothing here is proven.
115. **The long session on Gemini 3.8 Flash, heavy turns, alone and with AXI: drift, hallucination, speed, latency, tokens and an energy stand-in (22 Oct)** - the owner asked for the ultimate long-horizon test on Gemini 3.8 Flash, heavy turns, measured as a company that runs the model counts it, with and without AXI. **Status: run once on Colab by the owner on 22 Oct; Result written; 17 of the 20 lines read yes, headline MIXED.** Page `docs/GEMINI_LONG_TEST.md` (locked head, Result below), module `python/axi/experiments/gemini_long.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_gemini_long.ipynb`, saved run `docs/results/axi_gemini_long_session_seed20261111_default.json`. With AXI the held state was exact on all 1,500 steps at a flat prompt; the kept chat (457 turns to 935,000 tokens) gave exact replies for its first 222 turns, then slipped on 15 turns, its held state was corrupted from turn 287 on, and its seconds a call and time to the first token grew with the prompt; the depth probe (perfect history, 10 turns at five depths) was exact everywhere. 116. **A long conversation in plain sentences on Gemini 3.8 Flash: the state is ambiguous text, not a graph; drift, distance from the true list, speed, latency, tokens and an energy stand-in, alone and with AXI (22 Oct)** - the answer to the objection that Test 22 (item 115) kept a rigid dependency graph and that real conversational work has a state that is ambiguous text. 400 turns of a script-written conversation about a short list of items (owner, day, amount, status): new items, changes, corrections, loose references ("the last one we added", "whatever Priya is handling", "the same day as ..."), items dropped, 30 sentences of office chatter around every message, and a question every tenth turn (old and new facts, a fact never given, a reference that fits two items). Every reply is marked by exact match with the script's hidden true list. Arms: K (kept chat, blind apply), A (the list held by the framework and shown in the order added, a check before a reply touches it, up to two re-asks) and D (depth probe with a perfect history). **Status: run once on Colab by the owner (22 Oct); Result written; 15 of the 24 lines read yes, headline AXI HOLDS ON FUZZY TEXT.** The kept chat did not drift or hallucinate in 400 turns (398 of 400 replies exact, none corrupted, old facts within the page's threshold), but one wrong change at turn 191 stayed in the state it would have held for 140 turns, so the held list was exact on 65.0 % of the turns; with AXI the held list was exact on all 400 steps at about 1,177 prompt tokens a step (85 times fewer than the kept chat) and a flat first token (2.41 s against 5.57 s past 150,000 tokens). AXI was not faster at a short prompt (lines 9, 12 to 16, 18, 21 and 24 read NO). Page `docs/GEMINI_FUZZY_TEST.md` (24 reading lines and probabilities locked before any call), module `python/axi/experiments/gemini_fuzzy.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_gemini_fuzzy.ipynb` (pilot first, prices typed in by the owner, dollar budget and token caps). Not shown: joules (the API gives none), free-form sentences as state, any other model, job or company. **Checked here:** test 142 (the instrument on a stand-in model) and test 145 (the Result recounted from the saved file `docs/results/axi_gemini_fuzzy_seed20261122_default.json`). The ARC-AGI-3 test follows it.
117. **Gemini 3.8 Flash on ARC-AGI-3 games, alone and with AXI: tokens, latency, speed, wasted moves, illegal moves and loops (22 Oct)** - the owner asked for an ARC-AGI-3 test after the free probe notebook (`python/colab/axi_arc_probe.ipynb`) showed 25 public games, 8 actions and a game step of 0.4 to 1.0 ms. Four public games, 60 moves each, the same model and the same moves in two arms: K (one chat per game, every screen and reply kept and sent again) and A (nothing kept; each move the current screen plus a Record the framework keeps exactly from the game's own screens, and a check that stops a move the game does not offer, with up to two re-asks). This is a test of what the framework's exact record and check do to the cost and steadiness of the model on a hard game, **not** of how well the model solves new games and not a leaderboard entry; zero levels in both arms is a likely outcome and the page says so beforehand. **Status: run once on Colab by the owner (23 Oct); Result written; 11 of the 19 lines read yes, headline MIXED (line 8 yes, line 13 NO).** AXI sent 13.2 times fewer tokens (1.95 million against 25.7 million) and ended no move with an unusable reply (the kept chat: 13 of 240, mostly empty replies after long thinking), with 95.0 % effective moves against 87.5 %; it was slower (28.7 s a move against 15.9 s) and completed 1 level against 2, because the model thought 3,863 tokens a move with AXI against 1,714. Line 6 (the instrument line) read NO and the page's rule applied: the cause is written in the Result. Page `docs/GEMINI_ARC_TEST.md` (19 reading lines and probabilities locked before any call), module `python/axi/experiments/gemini_arc.py` (imports no ARC package; the notebook hands it the toolkit's `make`), notebook `python/colab/axi_gemini_arc.ipynb` (pilot first, prices typed in by the owner, dollar budget and token caps). Not shown: joules (the API gives none), the leaderboard's score, other games, models or move budgets, and whether the Record is the best possible one. **Checked here:** test 143 (the instrument on a stand-in game) and test 145 (the Result recounted from the saved file `docs/results/axi_gemini_arc_default.json`).
118. **Gemini 3.8 Flash on ARC-AGI-3 games with a host-owned explorer (arm B), against arms A and K of Test 24 (23 Oct)** - after Test 24 (arm A: 240 moves, every move legal, one level in one of four games, but one action sent 34 times of 60 in one game and about 29 seconds a move) the owner shared notes naming further structural layers (an invariant address frame, typed operators, a conductor, a progress score Q = O/M, boundary completion for grid objects) and asked what could help. Whether the names match Sir's writing is for Sir to confirm. The notes were written for editing a grid toward a known target; ARC-AGI-3 offers only the games' own actions and no target, so the test takes what fits: an object view the framework computes from the game's frames, a list of the moves not yet tried from the exact screen, and a conductor that plays an untried move itself (no call to the model) when the last 4 moves found no new screen or the screen has been seen 3 times; Q is a reading only (new screens per 1,000 computed tokens). Typed operators and boundary completion are not used. The Record, the check and the re-asks of arm A and the framework's core are unchanged, and the model's thinking stays at its default. Same four games and 60 moves as Test 24; only arm B is run, arms A and K are read from the rows Test 24 saved. **Status: run once on Colab by the owner (24 Oct); Result written; 10 of the 17 lines read yes, 6 NO, 1 not read, headline MIXED.** Arm B (object view and untried list) gave 95.4 % effective moves against 95.0 %, the same level count as arm A (1, in a different game), 22 % fewer thinking tokens a call and 1.2 times the effective moves an hour, below the page's thresholds; the conductor never fired (0 of 240 moves played by the framework), so it is untested. Page `docs/GEMINI_ARC_B_TEST.md` (17 reading lines and probabilities locked before any call), module `python/axi/experiments/gemini_arc_b.py`, notebook `python/colab/axi_gemini_arc_b.ipynb` (pilot first, prices typed in by the owner, dollar budget and token cap). Not shown: how well the model solves new games, the leaderboard's score, joules, other explorers or stall rules. **Checked here:** test 144 (the instrument on a stand-in game) and test 145 (the Result recounted from the saved file `docs/results/axi_gemini_arc_b_default.json`).
119. **The technical reference and the company-side README (24 Oct)** - the owner asked for the fuzzy-state and ARC results to be packed into the repository, for an appraisal in the README from the point of view of a company that runs the model (what AXI changes and, with the same weight, what it does not), and for one long, fully technical document on AXI. `docs/AXI_TECHNICAL_REFERENCE.md` describes the architecture as the code has it (state, delta language, strict parser, gate, diagnostics and repair, scope, runtime, pager, grounding) with the measured results of tests 22 to 25 in tables generated from the saved files, the method (locked pages, hashes, recount tests), the limits and what is not shown; the README section `fuzzy-and-arc` is generated from the same figures. The architecture is Rajnish Choubey's; the code, the tests and the measurements are the owner's with an AI assistant as a tool. **Checked here:** test 145 (`python/tests/test_arc_fuzzy_results.py`: the three Results, the README section and the measured tables of the reference are the render of the saved files; the wording rules).
120. **The cost of being wrong (24 Oct)** - after the discussion of what drift, extra tokens and latency cost a company that runs the model, the owner asked for it to be written down from the company's side. `docs/COST_OF_BEING_WRONG.md` is generated by `python/axi/experiments/cost_of_wrong.py` from the saved files of tests 22 and 23 (no new run, no call): the turns that ended with a wrong held state and what they used (computed tokens and seconds, the stand-in for energy), what a step costs through the job and where the kept chat stops being the faster one, and the same job at other cache hit rates (assumed) and with other weights (assumed, no price list). The architecture is Rajnish Choubey's; the code, the tests and the measurements are the owner's with an AI assistant as a tool. **Not measured:** the cost of a wrong action in a real business, joules, memory per session or sessions per GPU, the cache rates of a real service. **Checked here:** test 146 (`python/tests/test_cost_of_wrong.py`: the totals equal the saved summaries, the figures recounted by hand, the page equals the render of the saved files, the wording rules).
121. **A wrap-around (torus) address frame in front of the gate (24 Oct)** - from an AI summary the owner pasted of two of Rajnish Choubey's articles, "The India of Zero & Infinity" and "The 99 Eyewitnesses of the Ramayana" (the articles were not read here), with code: an omnidirectional origin where every direction is presence, a fixed closed set of points (108) whose movement wraps, any point naming any other directly. The owner's rule: what AXI already has is left out (the exact state shown at every step instead of a history; the gate's independence of how cells are named, test of rows 79 and 80); what helps is built. `python/axi/engine/torus.py` is a geometry layer in front of the unchanged gate: strict or wrap addresses (a wrap is reported, never silent), offsets and distance on the ring, wrap-around neighbours as `adj` edges, a move written as an ordinary CICO delta that the gate decides and `invert` undoes, and shifts of a whole state round the torus. Run as pasted, the summary's code accepted an address outside its 108 points (200 written to point 92), a move onto an occupied point (the object there vanished) and a move from an empty point; here strict mode, identification and match refuse them. The 108-point complete mesh has 5,778 links, as the summary says, but needs a surface with 910 handles, not a torus (seven points is the most a torus carries fully joined); the torus is the address space. Page `docs/TORUS_FRAME.md`. **Not shown:** that a model does better on a wrap-around frame, anything about ARC; no model is called. **Checked here:** test 147 (`python/tests/test_torus_frame.py`).
122. **Many eyewitnesses, one entity: a witness quorum and a field of descriptions (24 Oct)** - from Rajnish Choubey's article "The 99 Eyewitnesses of the Ramayana" (the owner pasted its text): ninety-nine figures each describe Sita by one adjective, and all of them together pin her down. Read for structure, most of the article is already AXI (the state kept and the history dropped; writes without locks; one shared ledger for many agents, item 100; canonical forms); what AXI already has is left out. Two structures are new and built in `python/axi/engine/witnesses.py`, with the core unchanged: a **witness quorum** (several independent proposals for one request; a change is committed only when enough of them proposed exactly the same canonical change; a tie or too few commits nothing), aimed at the gate's one blind spot, a change that is legal but wrong; and a **field of descriptions** (an item named by where several facts meet: one fit is bound, several ambiguous, none unresolved; ambiguous and unresolved apply nothing). Offline on the Test 23 stream the host bound 23 of 23 owner references and flagged 2 of 2 ambiguous questions (the model also got them all). Under independence, three witnesses at the 4,000-step run's slip rate leave about 0.04 % wrong; samples of one model are not independent, so that is a ceiling. Page `docs/WITNESSES.md`. **Not shown:** any gain on a model, or the cost in calls; no model is called. **Checked here:** test 148 (`python/tests/test_witnesses.py`).
